NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
The Applicant, Novartis Pharmaceuticals Corporation, submitted NDA 218436 for remibrutinib
(tradename: RHAPSIDO), an oral inhibitor of Bruton’s tyrosine kinase (BTK), for “the treatment
of chronic spontaneous urticaria (CSU) in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1antihistamine treatment” at a proposed dosage of 25 mg orally twice daily. Because the
Applicant redeemed a priority review voucher, the NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary
review team on a priority review timeline. Each team recommends approval of remibrutinib for
the proposed dosage and indication.
To support the NDA for remibrutinib, the Applicant submitted results from two adequate and
well-controlled clinical investigations, CLOU064A2301 (REMIX-1) and CLOU064A2302
(REMIX-2), hereafter referred to as Trials A2301 and A2302. Trials A2301 and A2302 were
replicate phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials that enrolled 925 adults to
evaluate remibrutinib 25 mg orally twice daily over 52 weeks. Both trials demonstrated
statistically and clinically significant improvements in patient-reported outcomes (PROs) of itch
and hives.
The available safety data were adequate for review and the observed safety profile is acceptable
for its intended use in antihistamine-refractory CSU patients. The most common adverse
reactions were nasopharyngitis, bleeding (petechiae, contusion, ecchymosis), headache, nausea,
and abdominal pain. Bleeding reactions were mild or moderate in severity, and a Warning and
Precaution for bleeding risk was added to labeling.
The submitted clinical program is adequate to support the efficacy and safety of remibrutinib at
the approved recommended dosage for the treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain
symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment. The clinical review team concludes that the
improvement in itch and hives scores observed in the pivotal trials are clinically meaningful
treatment benefits of remibrutinib that outweigh the potential risks and that remibrutinib will
provide a novel oral treatment option in the armamentarium of CSU therapies. The NDA meets
all applicable chemistry, manufacturing, and controls standards to support the purported identity,
strength, quality, and purity of the remibrutinib drug product. The NDA includes appropriate
preapproval nonclinical and clinical pharmacology studies. Postmarketing requirement (PMR)
studies will be issued for pediatric studies to evaluate remibrutinib in children 12 to 17 years of
age and 6 to 11 years of age with CSU, including a nonclinical juvenile toxicity study in rats, a
pregnancy exposure registry, a pregnancy claims database retrospective cohort study, and a
clinical lactation study.
The regulatory action for this application is Approval.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigations.
To support the proposed indication for remibrutinib, the Applicant completed two replicate,
adequate and well-controlled, pivotal trials, A2301 (REMIX-1) and A2302 (REMIX-2). The
trials evaluated the efficacy and safety of remibrutinib in 912 adult subjects with CSU who
remained inadequately controlled despite treatment with second-generation H1-antihistamines.
The study design consisted of a 52-week treatment period, including a 24-week double-blind,
placebo-controlled treatment phase followed by a 28-week open-label period where all subjects
received remibrutinib, and concluded with a 4-week treatment-free follow-up period. A total of
925 subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily or placebo across
both trials, with 470 subjects in Trial A2301 and 455 subjects in Trial A2302. Efficacy results
were based on the 912 subjects who were treated in the 24-week blinded, controlled treatment
period. The coprimary efficacy endpoints were the absolute change from baseline in weekly itch
severity score (ISS7) and weekly hives severity score (HSS7) at Week 12. The ISS7 (range 0 to
21) was defined as the sum of the daily itch severity scores (range 0 to 3) recorded over a 7-day
period. The HSS7 (range 0 to 21) was defined as the sum of the daily hive severity scores (range
0 to 3) recorded over a 7-day period. The key secondary endpoint was absolute change from
baseline in weekly urticaria activity score (UAS7) at Week 12. The UAS7 (range 0 to 42) was a
composite of the ISS7 and HSS7. Higher scores indicate greater symptom severity with a score
of 0 reflecting absence of symptoms. Efficacy outcomes based on the UAS7 and its ISS7 and
HSS7 components are direct, relevant measures of how patients with CSU feel and are
established PRO endpoints for CSU development programs.
In Trial A2301, remibrutinib demonstrated statistically significant superiority over placebo with
treatment differences of −2.63 (95% CI: −3.70 −1.56; p<0.001) for ISS7 and −3.61 (95% CI:
−4.85, −2.36; p<0.001) for HSS7 at Week 12. Trial A2302 showed similar results with treatment
differences of −3.23 (95% CI: −4.29, −2.16; p<0.001) for ISS7 and −4.47 (95% CI: −5.71,
−3.23; p<0.001) for HSS7 at Week 12. Likewise, the change from baseline in UAS7 at Week 12
in Trials A2301 and A2302 were −6.22 and −7.68, respectively, compared to placebo and both
statistically significant.
Efficacy was further supported by statistically significant and clinically meaningful results of
secondary endpoints assessed by responder analyses on the UAS7, including UAS7 ≤6 and
UAS7 =0. Compared to placebo, a higher proportion of subjects treated with remibrutinib
achieved UAS7 ≤6 at Week 12 (50% versus 25% in A2301; 47% versus 20% in A2302) and
complete absence of itch and hives (UAS7 =0) at Week 12 (31% versus 11% in A2301; 28%
versus 7% in A2302). Further, the onset of statistically significant differences in the treatment
effect were observed at Week 2 with 34% and 30% of subjects in the remibrutinib group
achieving UAS7 ≤6 compared to 3% and 6% of subjects in the placebo group in Trials A2301
and A2302, respectively.

Activation of mast cells and basophils is central to the pathogenesis of

CSU is a nonprogressive, self-limited disorder in most patients, with a

Current guideline recommended first-line treatment options for CSU
include H1-antihistamines (H1AH) at approved doses and up to 4-fold

therapies (e.g., cyclosporine, H2AH hydroxychloroquine, leukotriene

systemic corticoidsteroids may also be used periodically to gain short
term control of worsening symptoms. The alternative off-label
therapies have significant adverse event profiles.

Conclusions and Reasons

The NDA safety database includes approximately 2900 subjects who
were exposed to at least one dose of remibrutinib. Trials A2301 and
A2302 provide the primary safety database for the proposed dosage
and indication, and consists of 912 total subjects, with 868 treated
with remibrutinib, of whom 772 were exposed for ≥24 weeks and 438
exposed for ≥52 weeks.
There were no deaths in the clinical development program. Nonfatal
serious adverse events (SAEs) and adverse events leading to
treatment discontinuation were infrequent.
The most common treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were
nasopharyngitis, hemorrhage, headache, nausea, and abdominal
pain.
Remibrutinib treatment was associated with an increased risk of
bleeding, likely due to its pharmacologic effect on platelet function.
Bleeding reactions primarily manifested as petechiae, contusion,
ecchymosis, or purpura. A small number of subjects discontinued
treatment due to bleeding reactions; however, all reactions were
considered mild or moderate in severity. There was no correlation
between increased risk of bleeding and thrombocytopenia.
Occurrence of other adverse events of special interest (AESIs) such
as infections, cytopenias, and hepatoxicity was similar between
treatment groups.
A dedicated hepatic impairment (HI) study revealed 2- to 3.5-fold
increases in remibrutinib systemic exposures across any degree of
liver dysfunction.
Remibrutinib is susceptible to CYP3A4-mediated drug interactions.
Moderate and strong CYP3A4 inhibitors increase exposure by 2- to 4fold, while strong CYP3A4 inducers decrease exposure by ~75%.
Due to its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to
impact the immune response to vaccinations. Although the Applicant
conducted a vaccine immune response biomarker clinical study, the
design and endpoints were inadequate to yield clinically relevant
results.

The safety database is adequate to assess the safety
of remibrutinib at the proposed dosage for the
proposed context of use.
A Warning and Precaution will be added for risk of
bleeding.
Because a single nominal dose and dosing regimen of
remibrutinib (i.e., 25 mg BID) was evaluated in the
pivotal phase 3 trials, there are limited clinical data to
guide dose adjustments for patients who are expected
to experience higher systemic exposures with the
recommended dosage. Given the benefit/risk
assessment of remibrutinib for CSU, a conservative
approach will be taken in labeling to recommend
avoidance of use in patients with any degree of HI and
with concomitant strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors.
Given the lack of clinical outcome data, a Warning and
Precaution will be added to recommend avoiding live
or live-attenuated vaccines in patients receiving
(b) (4)
remibrutinib
(b) (4)

The potential risks of remibrutinib can be adequately
addressed through labeling and pharmacovigilance.
No REMS is required.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
As described in Section 1.2, the Applicant has demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness
for remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily (BID) for the treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain
symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment. The safety assessment of remibrutinib revealed
no concerning safety signals that would preclude approval or require narrowing of the indicated
target population or adding a limitation of use. Potential risks can be addressed through labeling
and routine pharmacovigilance. Given that CSU is generally a self-limited condition with several
therapeutic treatment options, the team recommends adding a Warning and Precaution to the
United States Prescribing Information (USPI) for risk of bleeding and avoiding use in patients
with hepatic impairment or concomitant cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4 medication use due to
increased drug exposure and risk of adverse reactions. The Pediatric Research Equity Act PMRs
will be issued to conduct studies in pediatric subjects 12 to 17 years of age and subsequently 6 to
11 years of age, pending results of a repeat juvenile toxicity study in rats. As CSU
disproportionately affects women of child-bearing age, PMRs will also be issued to identify
unexpected serious risks of adverse maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes resulting from
remibrutinib use during pregnancy and unexpected serious risks of the potential presence of
remibrutinib in human breast milk resulting in effects on the breastfed infant.
In conclusion, the review team has determined that the benefit-risk assessment is favorable and
supports the approval of remibrutinib 25 mg orally twice daily for the proposed indication of
treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment.
Although the development program did not enrich for a population of subjects who were
refractory to all available therapies, there is a need for additional treatment options with varying
mechanisms of action and alternative routes of administration.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Chronic spontaneous urticaria, formerly known as chronic idiopathic urticaria, is a
nonprogressive, typically self-limited, condition characterized by recurrent pruritic hives
(wheals), with or without angioedema, for 6 weeks or longer without an identifiable etiology.
The urticarial lesions are typified by central swelling with surrounding erythema, pruritus, and
evanescent time course with individual lesions lasting up to 24 hours without residual scarring or
bruising of the skin. When present, angioedema manifests as episodic submucosal or
subcutaneous swelling, often affecting areas of the body with loose connective tissue in an
asymmetric pattern. CSU is distinct from urticaria and/or angioedema that occurs secondary to a
known trigger or underlying disease such as chronic inducible urticaria, hereditary angioedema,
mastocytosis, autoimmune conditions, etc. CSU is a common condition affecting approximately
0.5 to 1% of the general population with higher prevalence in adult females and peak incidence
in the 4th decade of life. CSU is a self-limited condition in most patients with a mean duration of
3 to 5 years and 2-year spontaneous remission rate of 30 to 50%; however, some patients may
experience symptoms for decades.
The underlying pathophysiology of CSU involves degranulation of cutaneous mast cells and
basophils leading to release of histamine and other mediators (e.g., cysteinyl leukotrienes,
prostaglandins, platelet-activating factor, proinflammatory cytokines) that cause vasodilation,
increased vascular permeability and pruritus, resulting in the characteristic wheals and
angioedema (Lang 2022). Urticarial skin lesions have a predominantly lymphocytic infiltrate
with occasional presence of eosinophils and neutrophils. Although mast cell activation is central
to the underlying disease process of CSU, the precise mechanisms, especially in the absence of
external triggers, remain incompletely understood.
Standard of care consists of nonsedating H1-antihistamines as first-line therapy, with dose
escalation up to the four times approved doses. Although most patients will experience a partial
or complete response with antihistamines alone, approximately 40 to 65% will remain
sufficiently symptomatic to require escalation in therapy. Current treatment guidelines
recommend omalizumab as an approved second-line therapy; however, about 30% of patients
have symptoms refractory to omalizumab. Dupilumab was recently approved for patients who
remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamines; however, the clinical program failed to
demonstrate a benefit in patients who are refractory to omalizumab. Cyclosporin, used off-label,
remains a third-line option in nonresponders, but requires close monitoring for adverse reactions.
Though there are safe and effective therapies for CSU, a sizeable subset of refractory patients
exist for whom additional therapeutic options would be beneficial.
Remibrutinib (LOU064) is an oral BTK inhibitor that inhibits mast cell and basophil
degranulation mediated by pathogenic immunoglobulin E (IgE) or immunoglobulin G (IgG)
directed against the Fc epsilon receptor-1 (FcεRI) or IgE. It blocks IgE- and IgG-mediated FcεRI
activation of mast cells and basophils and the subsequent release of histamine and other
proinflammatory mediators that cause itch, hives, and angioedema.
11
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
The proposed indication is for the treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain symptomatic
despite H1-antihistamine treatment at a proposed dosage of 25 mg orally twice daily. BTK
inhibition represents a novel therapeutic target for CSU.
Although remibrutinib does not have breakthrough therapy designation for this indication nor
meet criteria for a priority review, the Applicant submitted the NDA using the Rare Pediatric
Disease Priority Review Voucher (NDA 217759); therefore, this application was granted a
priority review. The following key efficacy and safety issues were identified during the review.

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The efficacy and safety review of remibrutinib for the proposed indication of CSU in adult
patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamines treatment is based on the results
from two identical, pivotal, phase 3 trials, REMIX-1 (CLOU064A2301; referred to as Trial
A2301) and REMIX-2 (CLOU064A2302; referred to as Trial A2302). The study protocols are
described in Section 6.2.2. A detailed clinical pharmacology review is available in Section 5.2.
Data from Trials A2301 and A2302 provided primary evidence evaluating the efficacy of
remibrutinib versus placebo in the treatment of CSU. These data are presented in Section 6.2.2.8.
For the evaluation of safety, data from Trials A2301 and A2302 were analyzed by the clinical
data scientist using the R programming environment and the clinical reviewer using JMP

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Remibrutinib is an oral, small molecule inhibitor of BTK, a member of the tyrosine kinase
expressed (in hepatocellular carcinoma) (TEC) family of nonreceptor tyrosine kinases consisting
of BTK, TEC, bone marrow X-linked kinase (BMX), interleukin-2 inducible T-cell kinase (ITK),
and tyrosine kinase (TXK). It inhibits both unphosphorylated and phosphorylated BTK with
nanomolar potency. BTK is expressed on mast cells, basophils, B-cells, macrophages, microglia,
and platelets. In B-cells, BTK is an important mediator of B-cell receptor signaling involved with
15
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
B-cell survival, proliferation, migration, and differentiation of memory B-cells in antibody
producing plasma cells. In mast cells and basophils, BTK is a component of FcεRI and Fc
gamma receptor (FcγR) signaling. In platelets, BTK is implicated in aggregation and clot
retraction.
CSU is a chronic skin disease characterized by the presence of chronic (lasting ≥6 weeks with
three to four occurrences/week) hives with no identifiable cause. Patients with CSU exhibit
increased numbers of dermal mast cells and circulating inflammatory markers, including
interleukin 6 (IL-6). In approximately 50% of patients with CSU, IgG autoantibodies against IgE
or FcεRI have been detected. In these cases, autoantibodies may activate mast cells and basophils
resulting in the release of mediators such as histamine. Remibrutinib is proposed as an inhibitor
of mast cell and basophil degranulation mediated by pathogenic IgE or IgG directed against the
FcεRI or IgE and blocker of IgE- and IgG-mediated FcεRI activation of mast cells and basophils.
In patients with CSU, remibrutinib prevents the release of histamine and other proinflammatory
mediators that cause itch, hives, or angioedema. In vivo, remibrutinib showed a response in two
animal models relevant to CSU, a mouse reverse passive Arthus model and a mouse passive
cutaneous anaphylaxis model. These are models of skin mast cell degranulation induced by
FcγR- and Fc epsilon receptor (FcεR)-mediated hypersensitivity.
Established Pharmacologic Class Discussion
(b) (4)

Remibrutinib inhibits human BTK with nanomolar
potency (half-maximal inhibitory concentration [IC50]: 0.6 to 7.5nM) and is more selective for
BTK compared to the BTK-related enzymes TEC and BMX (IC50: 124 to 141nM). However,
clinical pharmacokinetic data from the proposed clinical dose of 25 mg BID suggests a
remibrutinib concentration of approximately 128nM, which is sufficient to potentially inhibit the
BTK-related kinases TEC and BMX (refer to Table 5 below). Therefore, the totality of in vitro
and in vivo pharmacology data submitted support the established pharmacologic class of
remibrutinib as a kinase inhibitor.
(b) (4)

In a phase 2 dose-ranging study (A2201), all remibrutinib dosages evaluated, ranging from 10 mg QD to 100 mg BID
demonstrated improvement over placebo in the primary endpoint of change from baseline to Week 4 in the weekly
urticaria activity score (UAS7). Notably, the treatment effect was comparable across all dosages remibrutinib with no

In healthy subjects, the highest dosage of remibrutinib evaluated was 600 mg QD for 12 days in Study X2101 (see
Section 14.2.1.1).
In subjects with CSU, the highest dosage of remibrutinib evaluated was 100 mg BID for 12 weeks in Study A2201
and 52 weeks in Study A2201E1 (see Sections 14.2.7, 17.3, and 17.4).

Dose proportionality

Including the double-blind and open-label period in the pivotal trials, subjects were exposed to remibrutinib 25 mg
BID up to a maximum of 61 weeks (see Section 7.5).
In Study A2201, remibrutinib steady-state Cmax and AUCtau increases in an approximately dose-proportional manner
over the dosage range of 10 mg twice daily to 100 mg twice daily (0.4 to 4 times the highest approved recommended
dosage) in subjects with CSU.

Drug Information
Following the administration of multiple doses of 25 mg twice daily, Cmax increases 1.6-fold and AUC0-4 increases
2.7-fold. The AUC0-4 at steady state was obtained from Study A2201 following the administration of 25 mg BID, while
the initial AUC0-4 was calculated from Study X2101 following administration of the first dose of 25 mg. Both studies,
X2101 and A2201, employed an identical hard gelatin capsule formulation and bioanalytical method. The median
body weight of subjects in both studies was 77 kg. Study X2101 was conducted in a generally healthy population,
whereas Study A2201 was performed on subjects with CSU. Note that there is accumulation despite the short halflife of remibrutinib. This is likely due to the distribution/disposition of remibrutinib in human body.
Following twice daily dosing of remibrutinib, steady state was achieved by Day 2.
The to-be-marketed formulation (film-coated tablet) was used in all phase 3 trials. The interim formulation used
during development was compared to the to-be-marketed formulation in a comparative BA study (X2105). Study
X2105 was conducted to compare remibrutinib systemic exposure between the interim hard gelatin capsule
formulation used in phase 1 and 2 clinical trials and the to-be-marketed film-coated tablet formulation. Relative to the
hard gelatin capsule formulation, the film-coated tablet formulation yielded 28% lower Cmax and 20% lower AUC0-inf.
Refer to Section 14.2.3 for details. These differences are considered small with no clinical relevance.
Absorption
The absolute bioavailability for the to-be-marketed film-coated tablet formulation was not determined.
Median Tmax is approximately 1 hour under fasted conditions.
No clinically meaningful food effect was observed.
Following the administration of remibrutinib 25 mg BID using the to-be-marketed film-coated tablet formulation to
healthy subjects, a standard high-fat meal delayed Tmax by approximately 1 hour compared to the T max in the fasted
state. In comparison to the fasted state, the Cmax was slightly lower in the fed state with a geometric LSM ratio (90%
CI) (fed/fasted) of 0.951 (0.763 to 1.19) and the AUC0-12h was slightly higher in the fed stated with a geometric LSM
ratio (90% CI) (fed/fasted) of 1.33 (1.19 to 1.49) (see Section 14.2.4).
Distribution
Based on population PK analysis, remibrutinib volume of distribution at steady state was 58 L (central compartment)
and 1180 L (peripheral compartment).
Remibrutinib plasma protein binding is 95.4%
Remibrutinib is a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp), but it is not a substrate for other tested efflux or uptake
transporters.

Characteristic
Mass balance results

Drug Information
Elimination
The recovery of radioactivity following oral administration was incomplete, with only 56.2% of the administered dose
being accounted for at the end of Day 8. The reason for this is unclear but the Applicant attributes this to technical
issues. The majority of the recovered radioactivity was excreted in feces (31.8%) and then urine (24.4%). The
recovery of intravenously administered [14C]-remibrutinib was nearly complete at the end of Day 4, with the majority
of the radioactivity also recovered in feces (67% to 71.7%). About 26% to 30% of the radioactivity was recovered in
the urine following IV dosing.
Following oral administration, remibrutinib was the predominant species in blood, constituting 16.7% of the blood
14C-AUC
0-24h. The unchanged remibrutinib in excreta represented 1.86% of the administered dose and none of the
metabolites accounted for ≥10% of the total. The amount of unchanged remibrutinib excreted in urine was minimal,
constituting 0.65% of the oral dose, suggesting negligible renal excretion of remibrutinib. Following IV dosing, the
amount of unchanged remibrutinib excreted in urine was 2.93%. In urine, none of the metabolites accounted for
>10% for the total. Remibrutinib is assumed to be completely absorbed (Fa >98%) and extensively metabolized, as
only 1.21% of the oral dose was excreted in feces as unchanged remibrutinib. Unchanged remibrutinib was not
detected in feces following intravenous administration. In feces, none of the metabolites accounted for >10% for the
total.

Clearance
Half-life
Metabolic pathway(s)
Primary excretion
pathways (% dose)

There was no specific distribution of drug-related components into blood cells following either intravenous or oral
administration of remibrutinib. The AUC0-inf of total radioactivity in plasma was marginally higher than that in whole
blood (14900 h*ng-eq/mL versus 12700 h*ng-eq/mL) following oral administration. The AUC0-inf of total radioactivity
following intravenous administration in plasma and whole blood were also comparable (16.9 versus 18.5 h*ng-eq/mL
in IV Arm A and 13.2 versus 12.8 h*ng-eq/mL in IV Arm B), indicating an equal distribution of total radioactivity
between plasma and red blood cells. For additional details see Section 14.2.2.
Based on population PK analysis, the apparent oral clearance of remibrutinib is 160 L/hr.
The elimination half-life of remibrutinib is 1 to 2 hours.
Remibrutinib is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4.
Following the oral administration of 100-mg radiolabeled remibrutinib, unchanged remibrutinib in excreta represented
1.86% of the administered dose (0.65% in urine and 1.21% in feces). Following IV administration of radiolabeled
remibrutinib, the amount of unchanged remibrutinib in urine was 2.93% of the dose with none detected in feces. No
metabolite in urine or feces accounted for at least 10% of total exposure following oral or IV dosing.

Characteristic
Body weight

Age
Race/Ethnicity
Renal impairment

Hepatic impairment

Drug Information
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
No clinically meaningful effect of body weight on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was observed.
The impact of body weight on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was assessed through the combined effects of
fat-free mass (FFM) and fat mass (FM) using population PK analysis. Overall, no clinically significant differences in
the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib were observed over the body weight range of 39 to 162 kg.
Population PK analyses indicated that age (18 to 80 years) does not significantly impact the pharmacokinetics of
remibrutinib in subjects with CSU.
Based on population PK analysis, no clinically meaningful effect of race/ethnicity (59.3% non-Asian, 8.8% Mainland
Chinese, 12.2% Japanese, and 19.7% other Asian) on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was observed.
No dosage adjustment is recommended in patients with mild, moderate, or severe renal impairment. Renal excretion
of remibrutinib was found to account for <1% of total blood clearance. In the mass balance study, the amount of
unchanged remibrutinib excreted in urine was ≤2.9% of the dose. In addition, population PK analysis indicated that
creatinine clearance, ranging from 23 to 275 mL/min, did not significantly influence the clearance of remibrutinib.
The use of remibrutinib should be avoided in patients with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment (HI) (ChildPugh Class A, B, or C).

Following the administration of remibrutinib at a dose of 25 mg twice daily, the geometric mean ratios for patients
with mild, moderate, and severe HI compared to subjects with normal hepatic function were 1.85, 1.65, and 1.99,
respectively, for Cmax, and 2.33, 2.3, and 3.49, respectively, for AUClast,ss (see Section 14.2.5).
Drug-Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of
Coadministration of remibrutinib at 100 mg with a single dose of 0.25 mg digoxin (a P-gp substrate) increased
metabolism or transporters digoxin Cmax by 2.1-fold and AUC by 1.4-fold (see Section 14.2.6.2).
system by remibrutinib
Coadministration of remibrutinib at 100 mg with a single dose of 10-mg rosuvastatin (a BCRP and OATP1B
substrate) increased rosuvastatin Cmax by 1.6-fold and AUC by 1.7-fold (see Section 14.2.6.2).
Remibrutinib at the recommended dose of 25 mg twice daily is not expected to have a clinically relevant effect on the
pharmacokinetics of midazolam (CYP3A4 substrate), combined oral contraceptives containing ethinylestradiol and
levonorgestrel (CYP3A4 substrate), tolbutamide (CYP2C9 substrate), caffeine (CYP1A2 substrate), and
coproporphyrin I (endogenous OATP1B substrate) based on in vivo DDI studies conducted with high doses of
remibrutinib (100 mg) and physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) simulations. Refer to Sections 14.2.6 and
14.5.3 for details.

Remibrutinib Cmax increased by 3.3-fold and AUC increased by 4.3-fold following concomitant administration with

(see Section 14.2.6.1).

approximately 2.3-fold following concomitant administration with erythromycin (a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor)
500 mg four times a day for 7 days (see Section 14.5.3).
Remibrutinib Cmax decreased by 74% and AUC decreased by 77% following concomitant administration with
carbamazepine (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) 300 mg twice daily for 14 days (see Section 14.2.6.2).
Remibrutinib Cmax is predicted to decrease by approximately by 60% and AUC is predicted to decrease by
approximately 64% following concomitant administration with efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) 600 mg once
daily for 14 days (see Section 14.5.3).
No clinically relevant changes in remibrutinib exposure were observed among subjects with CSU who were
concomitantly taking acid-reducing agents.
The risks and benefits of coadministration of antithrombotic agents with remibrutinib should be considered due to the
potential platelet inhibitory effect of remibrutinib as a BTK inhibitor. No PK data are available for the coadministered
anticoagulants in phase 3 trials. Exposure-response analysis indicated no significant relationship between the
incidence of bleeding and increasing remibrutinib concentration at steady state.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
Supporting Evidence for the Selected Dose
Dose-Ranging
The selection of remibrutinib 25 mg BID dosing for the pivotal phase 3 clinical Trials A2301 and
A2302 was based on findings from a dose-ranging phase 2 study (A2201) (see Section 14.2.7).
In Study A2201, adult subjects with CSU who remained inadequately controlled on secondgeneration H1-antihistamines were randomized to receive remibrutinib at various dosing
regimens: 10 mg once daily (QD), 35 mg QD, 100 mg QD, 10 mg BID, 25 mg BID, 100 mg
BID, or placebo. The primary endpoint in Study A2201 was the change from baseline in the
UAS7 at Week 4 compared to placebo. The estimated difference in least-squares means relative
to placebo were as follows: −13.66 for 10 mg QD, −13.64 for 35 mg QD, −9.21 for 100 mg QD,
−10.55 for 10 mg BID, −14.58 for 25 mg BID, and −12.62 for 100 mg BID. Notably, all dose
groups demonstrated an improvement over placebo in UAS7 at Week 4. The treatment effect
was comparable across all dose groups evaluated in Study A2201 but was numerically highest
among subjects randomized to receive the 25-mg BID dosage. This finding is further
corroborated by the dose-response relationship, which demonstrated that all dosages lie on the
plateau region of the response curve (Figure 1). Importantly, the observed dose-response
relationship is defined based on improvement over the placebo as there is no apparent difference
between any of the administered dosing regimens, irrespective of dosing frequency.
Figure 1. Dose-Response Relationship Based on Change From Baseline UAS7 Score at Week 4 in
the Dose-Ranging Study A2201

Source: Study A2201 Clinical Report, Figure 11-1
The dose is displayed as total daily dose.
The dose-response curve shows the median value from 5000 bootstrap samples.
The shaded area displays the 90% prediction interval from 5000 bootstrap samples.
The dots with the error bar represent LS mean and 90% CI from the MMRM.
Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; CI, confidence interval; LS mean, least-squares mean; MMRM, mixed model for repeated measures;
QD, once daily; UAS7, weekly urticaria activity score

23
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
Similar results were observed for numerous secondary endpoints, including the change from
baseline to Week 12 in UAS7, the change from baseline in UAS7 over time, the proportion of
subjects achieving a UAS7 score of 0 at Week 12, and the proportion of subjects achieving a
UAS7 score ≤6 at Week 12. For all endpoints, improvement over placebo was demonstrated at
all dosages of remibrutinib, with numerically greatest response observed with the 25-mg BID
dosage (refer to Figure 11-2, and Tables 11-4 and 11-5, clinical study report for Study A2201).
Based on the relatively flat dose-response observed in Study A2201, the Agency recommended
evaluating more than one remibrutinib dose in phase 3 trials; however, the Applicant chose to
carry forward a single nominal dose and dosing regimen of remibrutinib 25 mg BID. Although
25-mg BID remibrutinib resulted in the numerically greatest response for the primary endpoint
and numerous secondary endpoints, Study A2201 was not designed to evaluate for significant
differences between the various remibrutinib dosing regimens. All dosages demonstrated
improvement over placebo with overlap observed in the treatment effects.
Phase 3 Trials
In pivotal phase 3 Trials A2301 and A2302, 25 mg BID was the only dosing regimen evaluated
in adult subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines. For full details on the
study designs and efficacy results from Trials A2301 and A2302, refer to Sections 6.2.1 and
6.2.2, respectively. In both trials, treatment with 25-mg BID remibrutinib yielded significant
improvements over placebo in the coprimary efficacy endpoints of change from baseline to
Week 12 in the HSS7 and the ISS7.
Exposure-Response Analyses
The 25-mg BID dosing regimen was also supported by exposure-response (E-R) analyses, which
evaluated the correlation between the average steady-state concentration of remibrutinib and
changes from baseline in ISS7, HSS7, and UAS7. Efficacy data and simulated average steadystate concentrations of remibrutinib at six dose levels (10 mg QD, 35 mg QD, 100 mg QD,
10 mg BID, 25 mg BID, 100 mg BID) plus placebo from the dose-ranging phase 2 study
(A2201) and the two phase 3 trials (A2301 and A2302) were included in the E-R analyses.
Analyses demonstrated a maximum induction effect (Emax) type of E-R relationship across all
measured endpoints, with the plateau of therapeutic effect achieved at relatively low drug
exposures (Figure 2). Refer to Section 14.5.2 for details. Results of the E-R analyses are similar
to the dose-response relationship observed in Study A2201. The E-R relationship appears to be
primarily driven by the difference from the placebo group as there was little difference in
efficacy across quartiles of average concentrations at steady state.

The E-R analyses for safety, based on data from the phase 2 study (A2201) and the two phase 3
trials (A2301 and A2302), did not identify a significant relationship between remibrutinib
exposure and adverse events (AEs), serious adverse events (SAEs), or AEs of special interest
(bleeding, cytopenia, and infection). Refer to Section 14.5.2 for details.
The available data indicate that the proposed dosage of 25 mg BID is appropriate for the
treatment of adult patients with CSU who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine
treatment.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Efficacy Trials, A2301 and A2302
The efficacy of remibrutinib for CSU was primarily demonstrated through two large, identically
designed phase 3 pivotal trials: CLOU064A2301 (A2301) and CLOU064A2302 (A2302). These
were global, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials that evaluated remibrutinib
25 mg twice daily versus placebo in adult subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1antihistamines. Both trials had a 52-week treatment period, consisting of a 24-week placebo26
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
controlled treatment period followed by a 28-week single-arm, unblinded treatment period. The
primary efficacy analysis was conducted at Week 12.

6.2.2. Trials A2301 and A2302
6.2.2.1. Design and Conduct, Trials A2301 and A2302
Given that Trials A2301 (REMIX-1) and A2302 (REMIX-2) were identical in design, the
protocols are discussed in tandem. Both were multicenter, 52-week, randomized, double-blind,
parallel-group, placebo-controlled phase 3 trials investigating the efficacy, safety, and
tolerability of remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily in adult subjects with CSU inadequately controlled
by H1-antihistamines. After obtaining informed consent, subjects entered a screening period of
up to 4 weeks during which subjects underwent an evaluation to determine their eligibility. The
screening visit included a physical exam, collection of demographic information, height and
weight measurements, vital signs assessment, and electrocardiogram (ECG) recording. Subjects
received their electronic (device) diary (eDiary) during the screening period, which was used
throughout the study to complete the urticaria patient daily diary (UPDD) containing the urticaria
activity score (UAS) and angioedema activity score (AAS).
Eligible subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either remibrutinib 25 mg or matching
placebo orally twice daily for 24 weeks. Following the double-blind, placebo-controlled
treatment period, all subjects received open-label remibrutinib for the remaining 28 weeks of the
trial. After completing the 52-week treatment period, subjects were followed for 4 weeks offtreatment. The study schematic is shown in Figure 3. The schedule of study assessments is in
Section 15.1.1.

6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trials A2301 and A2302
Key eligibility criteria for the trials are summarized below.
Inclusion Criteria


Male and female adult subjects ≥18 years of age at the time of screening.



CSU duration for ≥6 months prior to screening (defined as the onset of CSU determined by
the investigator based on all available supporting documentation).



Diagnosis of CSU inadequately controlled by second-generation H1-antihistamines at the
time of randomization defined as:
– Presence of itch and hives for ≥6 consecutive weeks prior to screening despite use of
second-generation H1-antihistamines during this time period
– UAS7 score (range 0 to 42) ≥16, ISS7 score (range 0 to 21) ≥6 and HSS7 score (range 0
to 21) ≥6 during the 7 days prior to randomization (Day 1)



Documentation of hives within 3 months before randomization (either at screening and/or at
randomization; or documented in the subjects’ medical history).



Subjects must not have had more than one missing UPDD entry (either morning or evening)
in the 7 days prior to randomization during screening (Day 1).

Exclusion Criteria


Clearly defined predominant or sole trigger of chronic urticaria (chronic inducible urticaria)
including urticaria factitia (symptomatic dermographism), cold, heat, solar, pressure, delayed
pressure, aquagenic, cholinergic, or contact urticaria.



Other diseases with symptoms of urticaria or angioedema, including but not limited to
urticaria vasculitis, urticaria pigmentosa, erythema multiforme, mastocytosis, hereditary
urticaria, or drug-induced urticaria.



Any other skin disease associated with chronic itching that might influence the investigator’s
opinion, the trial evaluations, or results, e.g., atopic dermatitis, bullous pemphigoid,
dermatitis herpetiformis, senile pruritus or psoriasis.



Use of prohibited medications (see Table 99).



Known history or evidence of ongoing alcohol or drug abuse within the last 6 months before
randomization.



History of malignancy of any organ system (other than localized basal cell carcinoma of the
skin or in situ cervical cancer), treated or untreated, within the past 5 years, regardless of
whether there was evidence of local recurrence or metastases.



Pregnancy or lactation.



Women of child-bearing potential,1 defined as all women physiologically capable of
becoming pregnant, unless using highly effective methods of contraception during dosing
and for 7 days after stopping of study treatment. Highly effective contraception methods
included:
– Total abstinence (when in line with preferred and usual lifestyle of the subject). Periodic

withdrawal were not acceptable methods of contraception.
– Female sterilization (surgical bilateral oophorectomy with or without hysterectomy), total

Uncontrolled disease states, such as asthma, or inflammatory bowel disease, where flares are

History or current hepatic disease including but not limited to acute or chronic hepatitis,
cirrhosis or hepatic failure or aspartate aminotransferase (AST)/alanine aminotransferase
(ALT) levels of more than 1.5x upper limit of normal (ULN) or international normalized
ratio (INR) of more than 1.5 at screening.



History of renal disease, creatinine level above 1.5x ULN, or estimated glomerular filtration
rate <45 mL/min (using the Cockcroft-Gault equation) at screening.



Evidence of an ongoing hepatitis C infection (e.g., defined by the detection of hepatitis C
RNA at screening) and/or an ongoing hepatitis B infection (defined by the detection of
hepatitis B virus surface antigen [HBsAg] and/or hepatitis B virus [HBV]-DNA at screening;
subjects who are positive for antihepatitis B core antibodies but who are negative for
antibodies against HBsAg and HBV-DNA can be included into the trial if they agree to
monitoring for HBsAg and HBV-DNA reactivation).



Known or suspected ongoing, chronic, or recurrent infectious disease including but not
limited to opportunistic infections (e.g., tuberculosis, atypical mycobacterioses, listeriosis or
aspergillosis) and/or known positivity for HIV infection. HIV antigen/antibody tests will be
performed to determine HIV status if required according to local regulations.



Use of other investigational drugs within five half-lives or within 30 days (for small
molecules) prior to screening or until the expected pharmacodynamic (PD) effect has
returned to baseline (for biologics), whichever is longer; or longer if required by local
regulations.



Previous use of remibrutinib or other BTK inhibitors.



History of hypersensitivity to any of the study treatments or its excipients or to drugs of
similar chemical classes.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
6.2.2.3. Study Treatments, Trials A2301 and A2302
Remibrutinib was formulated as 25-mg film-coated tablets (FCT). There was a formulation
change between phase 2 and 3 trials from a hard gelatin capsule to FCT; for additional
information on formulations evaluated in previous studies, see Section 14.2. The investigational
medicinal product (IMP) was packaged and labeled according to the International Council for
Harmonisation (ICH) good manufacturing process and good clinical practice guidelines. Subjects
were treated with one of the following IMPs according to the assigned treatment sequence:


Remibrutinib 25-mg FCT administered orally twice daily



Matching placebo FCT administered orally twice daily

Each subject was instructed to take one FCT in the morning and one FCT in the evening.
Compliance was assessed by the investigator and/or study personnel in the drug accountability
form at each study visit.
Permitted Interruptions of Study Treatment
Study treatment interruptions were permitted under predefined circumstances to manage specific
safety events. For bleeding event management, treatment interruption was required 7 days before
planned surgery with clinically significant bleeding risk. Following surgical recovery, subjects
could resume study treatment after a 7-day period, ensuring adequate hemostatic recovery while
minimizing treatment exposure gaps. In cases of hepatitis B reactivation, defined as new
appearance of detectable HBV-DNA or positive HBsAg, subjects were required to initiate
antiviral treatment according to local clinical practice standards and interrupt study treatment.
Study treatment resumption was permitted only after HBV-DNA and HBsAg reached
undetectable levels, ensuring viral suppression before BTK inhibitor re-exposure.
Discontinuations
Treatment discontinuation occurred when study medication was permanently stopped before the
protocol-specified completion date and was considered an intercurrent event handled with
treatment policy strategy.
6.2.2.4. Concomitant Medications, Trials A2301 and A2302
Concomitant Treatment
All subjects were required to maintain second-generation H1-antihistamine therapy at locally
approved doses as mandatory background therapy throughout the trial duration. Background H1antihistamine therapy was required to remain unchanged until the Week 12 visit to ensure
consistent baseline treatment during the primary efficacy evaluation period. Modifications to
background therapy were permitted after Week 12 only when medically required, specifically in
cases where adverse reactions were attributable to the background therapy as determined by the
investigator's clinical judgment.
Rescue Medications
Second-generation H1-antihistamines, different from those used as background medication, were
permitted as rescue therapy on an as-needed basis for subjects experiencing CSU flare-ups with
31
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
unbearable symptoms during screening, treatment, and follow-up periods. The daily dose of
these rescue H1-antihistamines was limited to no more than four times the locally approved dose,
following current urticaria treatment guidelines.
Oral corticosteroid use was prohibited prior to Week 12 to prevent interference with the primary
endpoint assessment. After Week 12, subjects could use oral corticosteroids as rescue medication
for unbearable CSU flare-ups, but usage was limited to a maximum of 3 days within any 30-day
period and no more than 9 total days after Week 12. The recommended corticosteroid dose was
20 to 50 mg prednisone or equivalent per day, consistent with current urticaria treatment
guidelines.
Intake of rescue medication as per protocol prior to Week 12 was considered an intercurrent
event handled via treatment policy strategy for data analysis.
Prohibited Medication
Biologic therapies were not permitted due to their potential to independently improve chronic
spontaneous urticaria symptoms and confound efficacy evaluations. Routine oral corticosteroids,
defined as more than three doses over a 5-day period, were prohibited, although oral
corticosteroids were permitted as additional rescue therapy for chronic spontaneous urticaria
after Week 12. For a full list of the medications prohibited during Trials A2301 and A2302 refer
to Table 99 in Section 15.1.2.
Composite strategy was applied in the setting of intake of strongly confounding prohibited
medication including:


Biologics treatment at any time before Week 12



Cyclosporin after Week 8 to Week 12



Systemic corticosteroids after Week 8 to Week 12

Measurements after this event were excluded from the analysis and were replaced with the worst
value of the endpoint.
6.2.2.5. Efficacy Assessments, Trials A2301 and A2302
Patient Reported Outcomes
All subjects were provided with an eDiary that contained the PROs that measured symptom
severity and effect on daily activities. The PROs used for primary and secondary efficacy
endpoints were captured in the eDiary that contained the UPDD and the AAS. Subjects
completed eDiary assessments twice daily for the UPDD and once daily for the AAS (if
prompted by presence of angioedema symptoms) from screening through Week 52 or until trial
discontinuation. Other PRO questionnaires were administered during trial site visits (or in the
eDiary at the scheduled time if unable to attend in person).
The UPDD assessed severity of itch and number of hives, use of rescue medication, sleep and
activity interference, angioedema occurrence and management, and healthcare utilization (see

32
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
Figure 65 for a copy of the full instrument). Relevant components of the instrument used for
efficacy endpoints included:


Itch severity score (ISS): Itch intensity was reported twice daily on a scale from 0 to 3
(0 = none, 1 = mild, 2 = moderate 3 = severe). The ISS7 was calculated by summing the
average daily ISS scores over 7 consecutive days (range 0 to 21).



Hives severity score (HSS): Hives severity was reported twice daily on a scale from 0 to 3
(0 = none, 1 = 1 to 6 hives, 2 = 7 to 12 hives, 3 = greater than 12 hives) (see Figure 65). The
HSS7 was calculated by summing the average daily HSS scores over 7 consecutive days
(range 0 to 21). The scoring system for hives has differed across CSU development programs
as some HSS instruments score hives as 1 = <20 wheals, 2 = 20 to 50 wheals, 3 = >50
wheals. The HSS in this trial utilizes a more conservative scoring system.



UAS: The UAS is a composite of the ISS and HSS. The UAS7 is the sum of daily ISS and
HSS scores over 7 consecutive days (range 0 to 42). The UAS7 is an established and
accepted PRO measure used to assess disease activity in chronic urticaria and considered fitfor-purpose for the proposed context of use in CSU (Hawro et al. 2018; Zuberbier et al.
2018).

The AAS, as shown in Figure 66, was completed daily and consists of five items, each scored 0
to 3 points. The daily score ranges from 0 to 15, with higher scores indicating greater disease
activity. The assessment began with an opening question from UPDD regarding the occurrence
of angioedema. When subjects responded “no” to this initial question, the AAS score for that day
was assigned a value of 0. Conversely, when subjects responded “yes,” they proceeded to
complete additional questions that evaluated the duration, severity, and impact of the
angioedema on both daily functioning and appearance. Each response field within the assessment
received a score ranging from 0 to 3 points, with higher scores indicating greater severity. For
this trial, AAS results were reported as a weekly angioedema activity score (AAS7), with
possible scores ranging from a minimum of 0 to a maximum of 105. Although this tool has been
described in the literature to assess disease activity in recurrent angioedema (Weller et al. 2013;
Zuberbier et al. 2018; Kulthanan et al. 2019), it has not yet been used to support regulatory
(b) (4) .
decision making
Other PROs captured in the trials included the dermatology life quality index (DLQI), EuroQol
5-dimension 5-level, work productivity and activity impairment, urticaria control test, patient
global impression of severity, and patient global impression of change. As these PROs are not
considered fit-for-purpose for the proposed context of use and/or were not proposed as
multiplicity-controlled endpoints or labeling claims, they are not discussed further in this review.
Biomarkers
Additional assessments included biomarker evaluations measuring total immunoglobulin levels,
peripheral blood B-cell counts and subsets, and the chronic urticaria index, an in vitro basophil
histamine release assay in which a subject’s serum is mixed with donor basophils and the
released histamine levels are measured through a quantitative enzyme immunoassay. A chronic
urticaria index value of greater than or equal to 10 presumably indicates that the subject has
either an autoimmune basis for their disease (autoantibodies for IgE, FcεRI, or anti-FcεRII) or an
alternate histamine-releasing factor.
33
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
6.2.2.6. Efficacy Endpoints, Trials A2301 and A2302
Coprimary Efficacy Endpoints
For the United States (based on FDA advice during development), the Applicant designated the
following as coprimary efficacy endpoints:


Absolute change from baseline in ISS7 compared to placebo at Week 12



Absolute change from baseline in HSS7 compared to placebo at Week 12

Secondary Endpoints


Absolute change from baseline in UAS7 compared to placebo at Week 12.



Proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 ≤6 (yes/no) at Week 12.



Proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 =0 (yes/no) at Week 12.



Proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 ≤6 (yes/no) at Week 2.



Proportion of subjects achieving DLQI =0 to 1 (yes/no) at Week 12.



Cumulative number of weeks that subjects achieve UAS7 ≤6 responses between baseline and
Week 12.



Cumulative number of weeks that subjects achieve AAS7 =0 responses between baseline and
Week 12.
6.2.2.7. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trials A2301 and A2302

The statistical analysis plan for Trial A2301 is Amendment 4 dated January 25, 2024, and
submitted to the FDA on February 5, 2024. The statistical analysis plan for Trial A2302 is
Amendment 5 dated January 25, 2024, and was submitted to the FDA on February 5, 2024. Trial
A2302 was identical to Trial A2301 in its design and analysis.
Analysis Sets
The three defined analysis sets were the full analysis set (FAS), safety analysis set (SAF), and
randomized analysis set (RAS).
FAS
The FAS included all subjects to whom study treatment was assigned by randomization but
excluded mis-randomized subjects. Mis-randomized subjects were defined as cases where
interactive response technology contacts were made by the site either prematurely or
inappropriately prior to confirmation of the subject’s final randomization eligibility and no study
medication was administered to the subject. The FAS was used for the coprimary efficacy
endpoints and all other efficacy analyses, unless otherwise stated.
SAF
The SAF that included all subjects who received at least one dose of study treatment, regardless
of randomization, and subjects were analyzed according to the study treatment received. The
34
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
actual treatment was defined as the treatment received over the trial. In case of error in
dispensation, the actual treatment corresponded to the treatment which was given most often.
Safety set for the entire study period includes the subjects from remibrutinib 25 mg and placebo.
The data from the open-label period for subjects who transitioned to remibrutinib 25 mg were
not included in the safety analysis. Safety analyses were conducted using the SAF.
RAS
The RAS consisted of all randomized subjects, regardless of whether or not they received a dose
of study drug, and subjects were analyzed according to the treatment they were assigned.
Intercurrent Events
For efficacy analyses, treatment policy strategy was used to handle the intercurrent events of
discontinuation of study treatment due to any reason and the intake of rescue medication, switch
of background medication, intake of other prohibited medication, or subjects noncompliant to
treatment prior to Week 12. The Applicant’s prespecified analysis used the composite strategy
with values replaced by the worst value of the endpoint to handle the intercurrent event of
strongly confounding prohibiting medication (e.g., biologics treatment at any time before Week
12, cyclosporin after Week 8, systemic corticosteroids after Week 8). However, the treatment
policy strategy for all intercurrent events was recommended to the Applicant during the planning
and is of primary regulatory interest. The results presented in this review correspond to the
Applicant’s prespecified intercurrent event handling strategy and comparisons of the analyses of
the two estimands are discussed where relevant.
Primary Analysis
The primary analysis used a linear mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) with data up to
Week 12 to estimate treatment differences for change from baseline in ISS7 score (respectively
HSS7) at Week 12. The MMRM included treatment group, baseline ISS7 score (respectively
HSS7), randomization strata variables (region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics), week, the
interaction of treatment by week, and the interaction of baseline ISS7 score (respectively HSS7)
by week as fixed effects. Repeated measures within subject were modeled using an unstructured
covariance of the error terms. The treatment difference at Week 12 was estimated using the leastsquares (LS) mean and 95% confidence interval, along with the corresponding p-value for
treatment comparison. The review team additionally considered a comparable analysis at Week
24, with details described in Section 15.1.4.2.
In the case of missing data prior to Week 12, the missing data were to be handled following the
missing-at-random (MAR) assumption. For the intercurrent event of strongly confounding
prohibiting medication, the values for all visits after the incurrent event were replaced with the
worst value of the endpoint. Treatment policy strategy was planned for the intercurrent event of
discontinuation of study treatment prior to Week 12 due to any reasons. The Applicant described
data after the intercurrent event of treatment discontinuation as retrieved dropout (RDO) data. If
no RDO data were collected after the intercurrent event, missing data were imputed based on the
following rules. For subjects in the active treatment arm, if ≥50% of subjects had RDO data at
Week 12, missing data were to be imputed using multiple imputation (MI) based on observed
RDO data in the active arm. If <50% of subjects had RDO data at Week 12, missing data were to
be imputed based on observed data in the placebo arm under the jump-to-reference assumption
35
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
using MI. For subjects in the placebo arm, if ≥50% of subjects had RDO data at Week 12,
missing data were to be imputed using MI based on observed RDO placebo arm data. If <50% of
subjects had RDO data at Week 12, missing data were to be imputed using MI under the MAR
assumption based on the observed placebo arm data.
Sensitivity, supplementary, and subgroups analyses were conducted to evaluate the robustness of
the primary analysis and the impact of baseline disease characteristics on efficacy. For details,
see Sections 15.1.4.3, 15.1.4.4, and 15.1.4.6.
Secondary Endpoints
A closed testing procedure was prespecified to test the coprimary and key secondary endpoints
with control of family-wise type I error at the one-sided alpha =0.025. The testing is strictly
hierarchical, so that null hypotheses can be tested along the predefined order at the level assigned
until a null hypothesis cannot be rejected, at which point the testing stops. If the null hypotheses
for the coprimary endpoints were rejected, then testing proceeded sequentially to test for the
following:


Change from baseline in UAS7 score at Week 12



Proportion of subjects with UAS7 ≤6 at Week 12



Proportion of subjects with UAS7 =0 at Week 12



Proportion of subjects with UAS7 ≤6 at Week 2



Proportion of subjects with DLQI =0/1 at Week 12



Cumulative number of weeks achieving UAS7 ≤6 response between baseline and Week 12



Cumulative number of weeks achieving AAS7 =0 response between baseline and Week 12

The analysis methods for the key secondary endpoints are as follows:
The treatment difference for change from baseline in UAS7 score at Week 12 was estimated via
a linear MMRM with the same specification as for the coprimary endpoints.
The number of subjects with UAS7 ≤6 at Week 12 was analyzed using a logistic regression
model including treatment group, region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics, and baseline
UAS7 score as covariates. The number of subjects with UAS7 =0 at Week 12, UAS7 ≤6 at Week
2, and DLQI =0/1 at Week 12 were analyzed similarly. However, DLQI is not considered to be a
well-defined and reliable tool to estimate clinical benefit for labeling claims due to issues
surrounding content validity and the interpretability of the complex, distal concepts captured in
the instrument.
The cumulative number of weeks achieving UAS7 ≤6 response between baseline and Week 12
was modelled using a negative binomial regression model with log link, and covariates of
treatment group, region, and prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics and the subject’s time in the
treatment period up to Week 12 (natural log of proportion of time in treatment period, i.e.,
natural log of number of weeks with the response variable in treatment period/12 weeks) as an
offset variable. The cumulative number of weeks achieving AAS7 =0 response between baseline
and Week 12 was analyzed similarly.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
6.2.2.8. Results of Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
This section summarizes the subject disposition, baseline demographics and clinical
characteristics, and primary and key secondary efficacy results from Trial A2301 to support the
efficacy of remibrutinib in subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines.
6.2.2.8.1. Subject Disposition, Trials A2301 and A2302
A total of 925 subjects were enrolled across both pivotal phase 3 trials, with 912 subjects
comprising the efficacy (FAS) and safety (SAF) analysis populations. Trial A2301 enrolled 470
subjects (313 randomized to remibrutinib 25 mg BID and 157 to placebo), while Trial A2302
enrolled 455 subjects (300 randomized to remibrutinib 25 mg BID and 155 to placebo) as shown
in Table 8. Some subjects were enrolled and randomized in error (e.g., failed to meet eligibility
criteria), and therefore included in the randomized analysis set but excluded from the FAS and
SAF populations because no IMP was administered. The trials utilized identical randomized,
double-blind, placebo-controlled designs with a 2:1 randomization ratio favoring the active
treatment arm.

6.2.2.8.2. Baseline Demographics and Clinical
Characteristics, Trials A2301 and A2302
The baseline demographics of the FAS population in Trials A2301 and A2302 are shown in
Table 10. The study populations were representative of the intended CSU patient population who
are predominantly middle-aged females, and there were no major differences between treatment
groups. The racial composition reflected the global nature of the trials, with subjects
predominantly White or Asian, with comparable proportions across treatment groups.
Approximately 20 to 25% of trial subjects were enrolled from U.S. sites. However, international
treatment guidelines for CSU exist and standard of care is similar across countries. Therefore,
results obtained from sites outside the United States should be applicable to a U.S. population.

Baseline disease characteristics of the FAS populations in Trials A2301 and A2302 are shown in Table 11. The study populations
reported a relatively high symptom burden at baseline as measured by baseline UAS7 scores, with no major difference between
treatment groups. However, an imbalance was noted in angioedema burden, with subjects in the remibrutinib group having higher
baseline angioedema activity as measured by mean AAS7 scores and fewer subjects being angioedema-free at baseline. Most subjects
in the trials were naïve to prior anti-IgE therapy, indicating that the study populations did not represent subjects refractory to all
available therapies.

6.2.2.8.3. Primary Efficacy Results, Trials A2301 and
A2302
Table 12 and Table 13 summarize the results for Trials A2301 and A2302 for the coprimary
efficacy endpoints of change from baseline to Week 12 in HSS7 and ISS7, respectively. After
adjusting for baseline HSS7 score, geographical region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics,
visit week, treatment by visit interaction, and baseline HSS7 score by visit interaction, the
estimated LS mean changes from baseline in HSS7 scores at Week 12 were greater in magnitude
in the remibrutinib groups (LS means =−10.47 [SE 0.40] and −10.47 [SE 0.39]), compared to
placebo (LS means =−6.86 [SE 0.55] and −6.00 [SE 0.53]) in Trials A2301 and A2302,
respectively. For HSS7, the estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were −3.61
(95% CI: −4.85, −2.36) and −4.47 (95% CI: −5.71, −3.23) in Trials A2301 and A2302,
respectively. Similarly, for ISS7, after adjusting for similar covariates, the estimated leastsquares mean changes from baseline in ISS7 scores at Week 12 were greater in magnitude in the
remibrutinib groups (LS mean =−9.52 [SE 0.34] and −8.95 [SE 0.34]) compared to placebo (LS
mean =−6.89 [SE 0.47] and −5.72 [SE 0.45]) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The
estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were −2.63 (95% CI: −3.70, −1.56) and
−3.23 (95% CI: −4.29, −2.16), respectively. The coprimary endpoints in both trials were
statistically significant with p-values <0.001. Results of analyses which correspond to the
estimand with treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events were substantially similar,
with p-values <0.001 and small differences in point estimates and confidence intervals (at the
most in the tenths place).

In Trial A2301, one subject in the remibrutinib 25-mg group experienced the intercurrent event
of intake of strongly confounding medication (see Section 6.2.2.4) prior to Week 12; results of
the primary and key secondary analyses were minimally impacted by the handling of this
intercurrent event with composite versus treatment policy strategy. In Trial A2302, one subject
in the remibrutinib 25-mg group and one subject in the placebo group experienced the
intercurrent event of intake of strongly confounding medication prior to Week 12; again, results
of the primary and key secondary analyses were minimally impacted by the handling of this
intercurrent event with composite versus treatment policy strategy.
Although the coprimary endpoints were evaluated at Week 12, the double-blind treatment period
continued through Week 24. It is of clinical interest to consider the comparison between
remibrutinib 25 mg and placebo through the end of the double-blind period for this chronic
condition. The review team used an MMRM analysis to estimate treatment differences at Week
24 and results are summarized in Table 100 and Table 101 of Section 16.1.1. For change from
baseline in HSS7 at Week 24, the estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were
−2.91 (95% CI: −4.16, −1.66) and −3.61 (95% CI: −4.92, −2.29) in Trials A2301 and A2302,
respectively. For change from baseline in ISS7 at Week 24, the estimated differences in LS
means relative to placebo were −1.81 (95% CI: −2.88, −0.73) and −3.03 (95% CI: −4.16, −1.90)
in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. Although the effect estimates are smaller at Week 24
than Week 12, both comparisons were nominally significant.
To further examine the treatment effect over time, plots of the weekly mean change from
baseline with standard error bars were produced. Figure 4 and Figure 5 depict the weekly mean
change from baseline in HSS7 in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively, through Week 52.
Figure 6 and Figure 7 depict the weekly mean change from baseline in ISS7 in Trials A2301 and
A2302, respectively, through Week 52. Both treatment groups in both trials experienced
decreases in mean changes in HSS7 and ISS7 from baseline and there is a separation between the
groups during the double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period.

6.2.2.8.4. Key Secondary Efficacy Results, Trials A2301
and A2302
Table 14 summarizes the results for the first key secondary efficacy endpoint of change from
baseline to Week 12 in UAS7 for Trials A2301 and A2302. UAS7 is the sum of HSS7 and ISS7,
and as such the results are closely related to the results for the coprimary endpoints. After
adjusting for baseline UAS7 score, geographical region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics,
visit week, treatment by visit interaction, and baseline UAS7 score by visit interaction, the
estimated least-squares mean changes from baseline in UAS7 scores at Week 12 were greater in
magnitude in the remibrutinib groups (LS mean =−20.02 (SE 0.72) and −19.41 (SE 0.70))
compared to placebo (LS mean =−13.79 (SE 0.98) and =−11.73 (SE 0.95)) in Trials A2301 and
A2302, respectively. In Trials A2301 and A2302, the respective estimated differences in LS
means relative to placebo were −6.22 (95% CI: −8.45, −4.00) and −7.68 (95% CI: −9.91, −5.46),
which were statistically significant. The analyses which correspond to the estimand with
treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events were substantially similar, with p-values
<0.001 and small differences in point estimates and confidence intervals (at the most in the
tenths place).

Table 15 summarizes the key secondary responder endpoints adjusted for geographical region,
prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics, and baseline score, which were all statistically significant
based on the prespecified testing procedure. For the responder endpoint of UAS7 ≤6 at Week 12,
more subjects responded to remibrutinib (49.8% and 46.8%) than placebo (24.8% and 19.6%)
with an estimated conditional odds ratio of 3.1 (95% CI: 2.0, 4.8) and 3.8 (95% CI: 2.4, 6.1) for
Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. For the responder endpoint of complete absence of itch
and hives defined as UAS7 =0 at Week 12, more subjects responded to remibrutinib (31.1% and
27.9%) than placebo (10.5% and 6.5%) with an estimated conditional odds ratio of 3.8 (95% CI:
2.2, 6.8) and 5.8 (95% CI: 2.9, 11.6) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. For these two
responder analyses, the corresponding analyses for the estimand with treatment policy strategy
for all intercurrent events were substantially similar, with p<0.001 and small differences in point
estimates and confidence intervals (at the most in the tenths place). For the responder endpoint of
UAS7 ≤6 at Week 2, more subjects responded to remibrutinib (33.7% and 30.0%) than placebo
(3.3% and 5.9%) with an estimated conditional odds ratio of 15.7 (95% CI: 6.3, 39.0) and 7.9
(95% CI: 3.8, 16.7) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The intercurrent event of intake of

Table 16 summarizes the results for the remaining key secondary endpoints adjusted for geographical region, prior exposure to antiIgE biologics, and an offset for subject’s time in the treatment period up to Week 12, which were both statistically significant based on
the prespecified testing procedure. The average cumulative number of weeks with UAS7 ≤6 up to Week 12 was longer for the
remibrutinib group (5.2 and 4.5) compared to the placebo group (1.9 and 1.4), with an estimated rate ratio of 2.7 (95% CI: 2.0, 3.6)
and 3.3 (95% CI: 2.3, 4.7) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The average cumulative number of angioedema-free weeks,
defined as AAS7 =0, up to Week 12 was longer for the remibrutinib group (8.4 and 8.8) compared to the placebo group (6.7 and 6.7),
with an estimated rate ratio of 1.3 (95% CI: 1.1, 1.4) and 1.3 (95% CI: 1.2, 1.5) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The analyses
for these two endpoints which correspond to the estimand with treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events were substantially
similar, with p-value<0.001 and minimal differences in point estimates and confidence intervals. While the cumulative weeks
endpoints show numerical advantages for remibrutinib, these findings raise several uncertainties regarding their clinical
meaningfulness and practical significance for informing prescribers. These endpoints do not address whether the periods of symptom
control were consecutive or intermittent; for small treatment differences, intermittent control may be less impactful to patients. The
Applicant proposed to include both endpoints in Section 14 of the USPI; however, the difference of approximately 3 weeks with
UAS7 ≤6 between the treatment arms is not informative beyond the other efficacy claims to be included in the label, such as the
proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 ≤6 at Weeks 2 and 12 and figures of ISS7 and HSS7 over time through Week 24. For
angioedema-free weeks, the difference between remibrutinib and placebo over 12 weeks represents less than two additional weeks of
freedom from angioedema symptoms. While statistically significant with a rate ratio of 1.3, this translates to less than two additional
angioedema-free weeks over a 3-month period. See Section 6.3.1 for additional discussion.
