6.2.2.8.5. Subgroup Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
Results of subgroup analyses by demographics and baseline disease characteristics demonstrated
similar treatment effects on the primary outcome compared to results in the overall study
population, all favoring remibrutinib (Figure 69, Figure 70, Figure 71, and Figure 72). Although
only a small subset of subjects had prior omalizumab exposure, an exploratory post hoc analysis
showed a favorable treatment effect in this subgroup (Figure 73). See Sections 15.1.4.4, 15.1.4.6,
16.1.4, and 16.2.1 for additional details.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Evaluation of Weekly Angioedema Activity Score
(b) (4)

Issue
(b) (4) cumulative
The
number of angioedema-free weeks (defined as AAS7 =0) from baseline to Week 12 in Trials
A2301 and A2302.

Background
Angioedema is present in a substantial proportion of subjects with chronic spontaneous urticaria,
affecting approximately 40 to 50% of cases, either in combination with wheals or rarely as the
sole manifestation. In contrast to bradykinin mediated angioedema (e.g., hereditary angioedema),
the angioedema in CSU is mast-cell mediated and typically shorter in duration (resolving within
24 to 72 hours). When present, angioedema associated with CSU generally responds to available
(b) (4)
therapies, but can be a more difficult to treat symptom in some patients.
(b) (4)

Assessment
The AAS is a patient-reported outcome instrument that quantifies angioedema activity over a
specified period. The AAS7 refers to the sum of daily AAS scores over a 7-day period,
analogous to the UAS7 used in urticaria. The AAS consists of five items, each scored daily (0 to
3 points per item, maximum 15 per day), with higher scores indicating greater disease activity.
The total AAS7 is the sum of daily scores for seven consecutive days (range 0 to 105). See
Section 15.1.3 for additional details on the content of the AAS7. Though the Division of Clinical
Outcome Assessment was not formally consulted for evaluation of content validity of the AAS7,
the review team generally considers the measurement tool to adequately cover relevant aspects
of angioedema and that an AAS7=0 is inherently meaningful.
However, the clinical significance of the difference in average cumulative number of
angioedema-free weeks (AAS7 =0) up to Week 12 between treatment groups is unclear. In the
remibrutinib arm, the average number of angioedema free weeks was 8.43 compared to 6.72
weeks in the placebo arm in Trial A2301, and 8.81 weeks versus 6.68 weeks respectively in Trial

51
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
A2302. Over 24 weeks, remibrutinib subjects had more angioedema-free weeks than placebo
subjects (17.43 versus 14.45 weeks in Trial A2301; 18.03 versus 13.97 weeks in Trial A2302).
Additionally, there were differences in the baseline angioedema burden between treatment
groups across both pivotal studies. In Study A2301, the mean AAS7 score was 27.94 in the
remibrutinib arm compared to 23.46 in the placebo arm, while in Study A2302, the remibrutinib
arm had a mean AAS7 score of 25.18 versus 19.59 in the placebo arm. This imbalance in
baseline favoring placebo added further uncertainty to the clinical interpretation of the results,
particularly when considering the proportion of patients achieving angioedema-free status
(AAS7 = 0) favored remibrutinib throughout the study by Week 24. In the remibrutinib group,
83.7% of subjects in Study A2301 were angioedema-free compared to 74.2% of subjects
receiving placebo, while in Study A2302, the rates were 85.2% versus 72.1% respectively. Given
the difference in baseline AAS7 scores, it cannot be discerned whether the increase in
angioedema free weeks in the remibrutinib group was due to the effect of drug, regression to the
mean, or baseline imbalance in angioedema symptoms.
While remibrutinib appeared to demonstrate statistically significant superiority over placebo in
reducing angioedema burden in subjects with CSU, the clinical significance of cumulative-free
weeks of AAS7 =0 is unclear and the difference in baseline angioedema burden between the
treatment arms limited interpretability. Additionally, the numerical difference between the
treatment groups is relatively small without obvious clinical significance.
Conclusion
Cumulative-free weeks of AAS7 =0 is of unknown clinical significance.

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
The nonclinical safety profile of remibrutinib supporting this NDA has been evaluated in a
comprehensive program that included repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs, reproductive
and developmental toxicity studies in rats and rabbits, and carcinogenicity studies in transgenic
mice and in rats. In addition, pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic studies were performed in
dedicated studies in mice, rats, rabbits, and dogs and as part of the repeat-dose toxicity studies to
support remibrutinib exposures and to compare pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics in
animals and humans.
Pharmacology studies show that remibrutinib inhibits human BTK in vitro with nanomolar
potency and is more selective for BTK compared to the BTK-related enzymes, TEC and BMX.
In a safety pharmacology battery of studies, remibrutinib had no effects in respiratory or central
nervous systems. Remibrutinib inhibited human ether-à-go-go-related gene currents in vitro (by
15.6%, 42.6%, 69.6%, and 88.9% for 0.3µM, 1µM, 3µM, and 10µM remibrutinib, respectively).
However, there were no cardiovascular effects observed in dogs at single doses up to
450 mg/kg/day remibrutinib. Pharmacokinetic studies were conducted in rats, mice, and dogs
52
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
with single oral and/or intravenous dosing of remibrutinib. Single-dose studies demonstrated
broad distribution of remibrutinib in all tissues, except for the brain, lens of the eye, spinal cord,
and white fat. Remibrutinib had rapid absorption with a time to maximum plasma concentration
(Tmax) of 0.25 to 8 hours. Binding of remibrutinib to plasma proteins was high (>90%) across all
species. Remibrutinib was shown to be primarily eliminated via metabolism and excretion
through the feces. No major metabolites were identified in humans.
The most frequently observed findings in the repeat-dose toxicology studies were observed in the
lymph nodes, thyroid glands, liver, pancreas, and blood. Similar findings have been observed
with other BTK inhibitors for the liver, mesenteric lymph node, and pancreas along with
hemorrhaging. Embryo-fetal toxicity occurred in pregnant animals administered BTK inhibitors
during organogenesis.
Lymph Nodes
Decreased cellularity and germinal centers were observed in the mesenteric lymph nodes and
gut-associated lymphoid tissue. In addition, sinusoidal red blood cells were observed in the
mandibular and mesenteric lymph nodes in the 39-week dog study. Similar findings were also
reported in the 26-week rat study. Sinusoidal red blood cells, sinusoidal dilatation, focal
angiomatous hyperplasia, diffuse reticuloendothelial hypertrophy/hyperplasia, and/or pigmented
macrophages were reported in the 2-year carcinogenicity study in rats.
Thyroid Gland
Follicular hypertrophy was observed in the 2-, 13-, and 26-week rat studies and in the 2-year
carcinogenicity study in rats.
Liver
Minimal hepatocellular hypertrophy, multinucleated hepatocytes, pigment, mitosis, periportal
vacuolation, and focal necrosis were observed accompanied by increased liver weights in the 26week rat study.
Pancreas
Minimal islet fibrosis characterized by multilobulated, irregularly outlined islets with
fibrosis/collagen/pigment was observed in the 26-week rat study and in the 2-year
carcinogenicity study in rats. BTK inhibitors have been associated with rat-specific pancreatic
lesions characterized by islet fibrosis, inflammation, and hemorrhage (Erickson et al. 2017).
Blood
Inhibition of collagen-induced platelet aggregation and prolonged bleeding time were observed
in both rat and dog toxicology studies.

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
Fertility was unaffected in rats and fetal malformations were not observed in pregnant rats.
However, in an embryo-fetal development study in pregnant rabbits administered up to
450 mg/kg/day remibrutinib during organogenesis, increases in the external malformations such
as eye opening, small jaws, and hyperflexion of forelimbs were observed at doses 141 times the
maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) on an area under the concentration-time curve
(AUC) basis. No increases in the incidence of malformations or variations were observed at
doses that were 23 times the MRHD on an AUC basis. In a pre- and postnatal developmental
toxicity study in rats, reduced litter, an increased number of stillborn pups, and an increased
number of pup deaths were reported. No adverse remibrutinib-related changes were observed in
maternal rats and offspring at doses that were 58 times the MRHD on a body surface area basis.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Remibrutinib is an oral kinase inhibitor, that primarily inhibits the activity of BTK, an enzyme
involved in the signaling pathways of B-cells and other immune cells. These inhibitors have been
evaluated in the treatment of hematologic malignancies and autoimmune diseases. A class effect
directly due to BTK inhibition theoretically includes increased risk of bleeding in both the
presence and absence of thrombocytopenia, suggesting an impaired platelet function as an
underlying cause. Off-target effects such as atrial fibrillation, hypertension, ventricular
arrhythmias, and increased risk of infection, including opportunistic infections, due to effects on
both adaptive and innate immunity have been reported particularly in those receiving less
selective BTK inhibitors for the treatment of hematologic malignancies and have been included
in the Warnings and Precautions section of the label.

54
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Remibrutinib is not yet approved in the United States or any foreign market for any indication;
therefore, no postmarketing experience is available for remibrutinib.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The clinical data scientists and clinical reviewer performed independent safety analyses using R
Studio 4.1.3, JMP, and Analysis Studio (Office of Computational Science) software programs.
All clinical studies conducted as part of the remibrutinib development program for CSU were
evaluated for safety; however, primary safety analyses were conducted in the SAF population
(total number of subjects [N]=912), defined as all subjects who received at least one dose of
study treatment, regardless of randomization, during the 24-week controlled treatment period in
Trials A2301 and A2302. Subjects were analyzed according to the study treatment received.
Given the identical study designs and similar safety findings in the individual trials, pooled
controlled safety data from Trials A2301 and A2302 are presented in this review. In addition,
supportive safety data from the open-label, uncontrolled treatment periods of Trials A2301 and
A2302, the phase 2 dose-finding Study A2201, the phase 2 open-label extension Study
A2201E1, the phase 3 open-label, single-arm study in Japanese adult subjects with CSU, Study
1301, were independently analyzed and reviewed (see Section 17).
The prespecified safety analysis plan and definitions were reviewed during development under
the IND and were acceptable to the clinical review team.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database is adequate to assess the safety of remibrutinib at the proposed dosage for
the proposed indication. No data quality or integrity issues were identified in the submitted
datasets or case report forms. Definitions of AEs and SAE were consistent with CFR 312.32.
Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were defined as AEs occurring from the first dose
of study treatment through 4 weeks (28 days) after the last dose, or until the end of the study
period, whichever came first. The Applicant determined AE severity according to the Common
Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE) version 5.0 toxicity grading scale. Verbatim
AEs were coded to standard AE terms using the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities
(MedDRA) version 26.1 for Trials A2301 and A2302. The Applicant’s mapping of verbatim
terms to MedDRA preferred terms for the events reported in the trial was reviewed and found to
be acceptable.
The primary safety database for remibrutinib at the proposed dosage of 25 mg BID consists of
the 912 subjects treated in the 24-week, blinded, placebo-controlled, treatment periods in Trials
A2301 and A2302. Additional support for the safety of remibrutinib, particularly for long-term
use, is derived from the 28-week, uncontrolled, open-label treatment periods in Trials A2301 and
A2302; a phase 2 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-ranging Study A2201 and
its 52-week open-label extension Study A2201E1, and a 52-week, open-label study in adult
Japanese subjects, Study A1301, all of which are discussed further in Sections 15 and 17.

Note, the Applicant also submitted a multicenter, open-label phase 3 trial, Study A2305, to

details.
The extent of remibrutinib exposure in Trials A2301 and A2302, as shown in Table 18, is
adequate to support the safety of a chronically administered drug. During the entire study period
for the pivotal trials, which extended up to 56 weeks including a 4-week follow-up period, the
remibrutinib 25-mg BID group achieved a median exposure duration of 52.0 weeks with a
cumulative exposure of 672 patient-years. Subjects who transitioned from placebo to
remibrutinib at Week 24 had a median exposure duration of 28.1 weeks with 134.48 patientyears of cumulative exposure.

7.6.1.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
There were no deaths reported during Trials A2301 and A2302, or in the entire clinical
development program.
7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events,
Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
The occurrence of SAEs during the 24-week placebo-controlled periods was relatively infrequent
and mainly single events with no major imbalances between treatment groups. A total of 20
subjects (3.3%) in the remibrutinib 25-mg BID group experienced SAEs compared to 7 subjects
(2.3%) in the placebo group. A subject-level listing of SAEs that occurred in the remibrutinibtreated arm are listed in Table 20. Based on a review of case narratives, most SAEs were related
to underlying medical conditions or coincidental medical events and appear unrelated to
remibrutinib.

7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading
to Treatment Discontinuation, Pooled Analyses,
Trials A2301 and A2302
The analysis of AEs leading to treatment discontinuation is a useful indicator of the tolerability
for chronic remibrutinib therapy for CSU. During the 24-week placebo-controlled period,
overall, AEs leading to study treatment discontinuation were generally low and balanced
between treatment groups. A total of 17 subjects (2.8%) in the remibrutinib 25-mg BID group
discontinued treatment due to adverse events compared to 9 subjects (2.9%) in the placebo
group. A subject-level listing of AEs that led to treatment discontinuation in the remibrutinib arm
is shown in Table 21. Bleeding-related events, though rare, including petechiae, purpura, and
contusion, were more commonly reported reasons for discontinuation in the remibrutinib group
than in the placebo group (3 [0.5%] versus 0). Otherwise, there was no clear pattern of organ
specific toxicity requiring discontinuation noted during the review with isolated AEs distributed
across multiple organ systems.

7.6.1.5. Common Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events,
Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
The overall incidence of TEAEs was similar between treatment groups, with 64.9% of
remibrutinib subjects (393/606) and 64.7% of placebo subjects (198/306) experiencing at least
one TEAE as shown in Table 22. The severity distribution was predominantly mild-to-moderate
across both groups, with severe adverse events occurring infrequently and in similar proportions
(3.0% versus 2.3% for remibrutinib and placebo, respectively). Moderate adverse events were
reported in 26.7% of remibrutinib subjects compared to 22.9% of placebo subjects, while mild
events occurred in 35.1% and 39.5%, respectively.
The most commonly reported TEAEs that occurred more frequently in the remibrutinib group
compared to placebo are shown in the tables below by individual preferred terms (PTs) and by
standardized groupings of PTs using OND custom medical queries (OCMQs).

7.6.1.6. Subgroup Analyses, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301
and A2302
In general, the AE profile across different demographic subgroups was balanced between
treatment groups as shown in Table 106, Section 17.1. Differences in frequency of AEs between
treatment arms was most prominent in the ≥75-year age group. However, the numbers of AEs
were small and were consistent with normal age-related expectations. Although the majority of
subjects were white or Asian making assessment of across all racial groups difficult, there were
no clear trends indicating increased AE risk based on race. Likewise, the safety profile was
consistent across sex, country, and ethnicity. See Section 17.1 for additional details.
7.6.1.7. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Trials
A2301 and A2302
Laboratory evaluation in the remibrutinib clinical development program included assessment of
hematology, clinical chemistry, and urinalysis parameters collected per the schedule of
assessments (Table 98). During the 24-week placebo-controlled period, the majority of newly
occurring laboratory abnormalities were Grade 1 or Grade 2 based on CTCAE criteria and were
generally balanced between the remibrutinib and placebo groups. Grade 3 and Grade 4
64
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
abnormalities were infrequent in both treatment groups, with no Grade 4 hematology events
reported during the placebo-controlled period and rare Grade 3 events that were similarly
distributed between groups. Laboratory findings related to liver function tests and cytopenias are
discussed in Sections 7.6.1.7 and 7.7.3.
The analysis of immunoglobulin levels revealed small decreases in median IgG and
immunoglobulin M (IgM) levels from baseline in the remibrutinib group (−0.64 g/L for IgG and
−0.17 g/L for IgM) compared to placebo (−0.45 g/L for IgG and −0.05 g/L for IgM); however,
the clinical relevance of these changes is uncertain as values remained within normal ranges and
decreased levels were not associated with increased infection rates (see Section 7.7.2) or other
clinical findings.
7.6.1.8. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled
Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
Because hepatotoxicity has been identified as a potential risk associated with other BTK
inhibitors, the assessment of potential drug-induced livery injury was of particular interest in this
application review. The exact mechanism of BTKi-induced liver injury is not fully understood
and remains a relatively rare TEAE. Factors that may increase the risk of liver injury with BTK
inhibitors include preexisting liver conditions, such as hepatic steatosis, and other medications.
Additionally, certain BTK inhibitors have boxed warnings to highlight the risk of hepatotoxicity
or list hepatotoxicity in the warnings and precautions section of the label.
The hepatotoxicity assessment for remibrutinib included a review of labs (liver enzymes—ALT,
AST, alkaline phosphatase, gamma-glutamyl transferase, and total bilirubin) and TEAEs with
systematic evaluation using both CTCAE grading criteria and evaluation of drug-induced serious
hepatotoxicity plots to identify potential Hy's Law cases.
During the placebo-controlled period, liver enzyme abnormalities were generally balanced
between treatment groups and primarily consisted of asymptomatic, isolated transaminase
elevations without concurrent bilirubin increases (i.e., ALT and/or AST without concurrent
increase of bilirubin). ALT elevations >3x ULN occurred in 1.2% of remibrutinib subjects versus
1.3% of placebo subjects, with most elevations falling between 3x and <5x ULN as shown in
Table 24.

Two subjects in the remibrutinib group experienced ALT elevations >5x ULN. The first case
involved a 62-year-old female with peak ALT of 5.3x ULN at Week 12 in Trial A2301,
occurring in the context of concurrent medications including amoxicillin/clavulanic acid and
nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, with rapid normalization following treatment
discontinuation. The second case was a 46-year-old female with peak ALT of 14.6x ULN at
Week 12, also associated with concurrent metamizole and prior amoxicillin/clavulanic acid use,
showing rapid normalization after treatment interruption and successful negative rechallenge
upon treatment resumption.
In evaluation of drug-induced serious hepatotoxicity plot analyses, no potential Hy's Law cases
(i.e., concurrent ALT or AST >3x ULN and total bilirubin >2x ULN) were identified in the SAF
population during the 24-week controlled treatment period; likewise, there were no potential
Hy’s Law cases observed in the entire 52-week treatment periods of the A2301 and A2302 or in
the supportive safety studies (see Section 17). Based on available data, the proposed dosage of
remibrutinib does not appear to pose a significant risk for drug-induced livery injury and no
additional monitoring beyond routine clinical care is required.
7.6.1.9. Vital Signs, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and
A2302
Vital signs were captured according to the schedule of assessments (Table 98) at each study visit,
approximately every 4 weeks. There was no clinically significant impact on vital signs with
remibrutinib or major differences between treatment groups. In addition, the Applicant
conducted a dedicated 24-hour ambulatory blood pressure monitoring study (A2305) in 144
subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines; the results showed no
substantial change in blood pressure with remibrutinib treatment (25 mg BID) after 4 weeks. See
Sections 15.6 and 17.6 and the Interdisciplinary Review Team consult memo dated June 13,
2025, for additional details regarding Study A2305.

RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Bleeding Risk
Issue
Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to cause bleeding.
Background
BTK inhibitors as a class, most notably ibrutinib, but also second-generation agents such as
acalabrutinib and zanubrutinib, have been associated with an increased risk of bleeding. The
bleeding risk may be due to BTK-dependent platelet dysfunction, which impairs platelet
aggregation and increases susceptibility to both minor and, less commonly, major hemorrhagic
events.
Assessment
The clinical review team consulted the Division of Nonmalignant Hematology to assist in the
bleeding risk assessment for remibrutinib. Rather than the Applicant’s custom query which
included PTs for platelet function (platelet aggregation abnormal, platelet aggregation decreased,
platelet aggregation inhibition, platelet dysfunction, platelet function test abnormal, and platelet
toxicity), the Division of Nonmalignant Hematology recommended using the standardized
MedDRA query or OCMQ for ‘hemorrhage’ as it only includes PTs for observed bleeding
reactions. Although abnormal laboratory tests may correlate with increased bleeding risk, this
relationship is not straightforward and varies depending on the specific test used, underlying
conditions, severity of abnormalities, and other hemostatic factors. As such, the assessment of
hemorrhagic adverse events should represent documented bleeding occurrences during trials, not
laboratory markers of potential bleeding risk.
An analysis of bleeding TEAEs using the OCMQ for ‘hemorrhage’ is shown in Table 25. In the
pooled safety population, bleeding reactions occurred in 9% of subjects treated with remibrutinib
compared to 2% in the placebo group during the 24-week controlled treatment period. Petechiae
(4%) and contusion (2%) were the most commonly reported TEAEs. No severe bleeding
reactions occurred. There were two bleeding events that were classified as serious, one occurred
in relation to an accident and was considered serious due to hospitalization and the second event
was hematuria that occurred in a subject with chronic urocystitis with squamous metaplasia.
Neither event led to treatment discontinuation, and both resolved with further intervention. In the
remibrutinib treatment arm, 0.5% experienced bleeding reactions that led to treatment
discontinuation, while none of these reactions occurred in the placebo group. Exposure-adjusted
occurrence rate (EAOR) for bleeding did not appear to increase over the entire 52-week study
period compared to the placebo-controlled period, suggesting the risk did not increase with
longer treatment duration and the first occurrence of bleeding events was highest within the first
4 to 12 weeks of treatment. Regarding dose-response relationships, the available data from
higher doses is limited. In the phase 2b extension Study A2201E1, subjects received remibrutinib
100 mg BID (four times the proposed therapeutic dose), and one SAE of melena was reported,
though this was attributed to concurrent bariatric surgery rather than drug effect (see
Sections 17.1, 17.3, and 17.4 for additional details).

To further explore the imbalance in bleeding TEAEs, the review team assessed whether there is
an exposure-response relationship for this safety signal. A comparison of bleeding TEAE rates
(using the Applicant’s custom query) by quartiles of remibrutinib average concentrations at
steady state (Cavg,ss) indicated no significant increase in bleeding events with increasing Cavg,ss
quartile. The 95% CIs for the proportion of subjects with bleeding overlapped between the
different exposure quartiles across three distinct safety data pools: E-R Safety Pool 1a, E-R
Safety Pool 1b, and E-R Safety Pool 2 (Table 26). Additionally, no significant difference was
observed in remibrutinib Cavg,ss between subjects who experienced bleeding and those who did
not, across all three safety data pools (Figure 8). Furthermore, exposure-response analyses for
safety indicated no significant relationship between the incidence of bleeding and increasing
remibrutinib Cavg,ss in any of the safety data pools examined (Figure 9).

E-R Safety Pool 1b: entire study on-treatment period in Trials A2301 and A2302, including all data collected under remibrutinib
treatment during placebo-controlled and open-label periods in Trials A2301 and A2302.
E-R Safety Pool 2: 12-week placebo-controlled period: Trials A2201, A2301 and A2302, data censored at Week 12 including all
active and placebo arms (follow-up limited by the duration of double-blinded placebo-controlled period in phase 2 Study A2201).

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
Notably, there are limitations to the above exposure-response analyses for safety. For example,
in Safety Pool 1a and Safety Pool 1b, both of which consider data from Trials A2301 and A2302,
only a single remibrutinib dosage of 25 mg BID was evaluated. Although Safety Pool 2 also
included data from dose-ranging Study A2201, which assessed dosages up to 100 mg BID, data
are only considered for the 12-week placebo-controlled period. Thus, these analyses do not
sufficiently inform the potential bleeding risk following chronic exposure to higher
doses/exposures of remibrutinib.
In the proposed USPI, the Applicant recommends withholding remibrutinib for 3 to 7 days preand postsurgery. However, with a half-life of only 1 to 2 hours and time to maximum
concentration of approximately 1 hour, the drug would be nearly completely eliminated within 5
to 10 hours (five to six half-lives). The Applicant provided justification for the interruption
period in a September 4, 2025 response to a labeling information request, stating that the 3-day
presurgical interruption accounts for the 48 to 72-hour platelet function recovery time following
covalent BTK inhibition. The 7-day consideration for postsurgical reintroduction is primarily
based on contemporary surgical literature recommendations for resuming treatment after major
surgical interventions, allowing adequate time for initial wound healing and hemostatic stability.
Regarding coadministration of remibrutinib with anticoagulants, no clinical data exists because
concomitant use was prohibited in the phase 3 clinical trials. Limited data were available for
antiplatelet agents (aspirin up to 100 mg daily and clopidogrel up to 75 mg daily were
permitted); however, the use of dual antiplatelet therapy, such as the combination of
acetylsalicylic acid and clopidogrel, was prohibited.
Conclusion
Remibrutinib is associated with an increased risk of bleeding reactions, likely due to its
mechanism of action and impact on platelet function; however, most events were related to
bruising/petechiae/contusion rather than overt hemorrhage. Reported bleeding TEAEs were
primarily mild or moderate in severity, with few requiring medical intervention or treatment
discontinuation. Supportive safety data suggest that there is no increase in risk over time with
extended use or higher doses; however, these data are limited. Given that CSU is a symptomatic
and self-limited condition with multiple available treatment options, the review team
recommends adding a Warning and Precaution to Section 5 of the USPI to inform prescribers of
the potential risk of bleeding reactions and need for consideration of the benefit/risk for
continued treatment if reactions occur. Given the observed safety signal for bleeding risk,
withholding remibrutinib perioperatively is a reasonable recommendation and the review team
accepted the Applicant’s justification. The team recommends advising prescribers to consider the
risks and benefits of concomitant antithrombotic use in Section 7 of the USPI given the absence
of clinical data from phase 3 trials.

7.7.2. Infection Risk
Issue
Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to cause immune compromise
leading to increased infection.

71
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
Background
BTK is a cytoplasmic tyrosine kinase and member of the TEC kinase family, selectively
expressed in cells of the adaptive and innate immune system, including B-cells, macrophages,
mast cells, basophils, and thrombocytes. In addition to its role for signaling through the FcεRI
for IgE and the activating FcγR for IgG, BTK plays an important role in signaling through the Bcell receptor pathway, theoretically affecting primary IgM and IgG responses. Clinical
experience with other BTK inhibitors revealed an increased risk for infections.
Assessment
During the 24-week placebo-controlled periods in Trials A2301 and A2302, the overall infection
rates (based on MedDRA system organ class ‘Infections and Infestations’) were comparable
between the remibrutinib 25-mg BID and placebo groups, with 33% versus 34% of subjects
experiencing infections, respectively. There was no difference between treatment groups in the
frequency of OCMQ terms for ‘viral infection’ or ‘bacterial infection’. The exposure-adjusted
incidence rates were similarly balanced at 95.6 versus 98.1 per 100 patient-years in the
remibrutinib and placebo groups, respectively. The types of infections observed were consistent
with those expected in the general population, predominantly consisting of respiratory tract
infections including coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) (10.7% versus 11.4%),
nasopharyngitis (6.6% versus 4.6%), and upper respiratory tract infections (3.0% versus 2.0%) in
the remibrutinib versus placebo treatment arms, respectively. Viral infections were the most
frequent, while bacterial and fungal infections were reported infrequently. No opportunistic
infections, as might be expected with the use of a BTK inhibitor, such as invasive aspergillosis,
cryptococcosis, or Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia nor encapsulated bacterial infections were
reported in the remibrutinib group.
The severity profile of infections demonstrated that most cases of infections were categorized as
mild-to-moderate in nature (67 to 68% mild, 32% moderate). Serious infections were infrequent
and occurred at comparable rates between treatment groups (0.8% versus 0.7%). There were two
subjects in the remibrutinib treatment arm who experienced a severe infection, bronchitis and
acrodermatitis, both moderate that did not lead to treatment interruption or discontinuation.
Treatment modifications due to infections were minimal, with only one infection leading to study
treatment discontinuation (COVID-19) and treatment interruptions occurring at similar rates
between groups (8.4% versus 7.9% of events). Long-term safety data extending up to Week 52
showed no increase in infection rates with prolonged exposure. The exposure-adjusted
occurrence rate decreased from 111.7 to 82.7 per 100 patient-years during the entire study
period, suggesting no cumulative infection risk with extended treatment duration (see
Section 17.1).
From an immunological perspective, while small decreases in IgG and IgM levels were observed
with remibrutinib treatment, these remained within normal ranges and showed no temporal
association with infection occurrence. Similarly, no correlation was identified between cytopenia
events and increased infection risk, further supporting the absence of clinically meaningful
immunosuppression.

72
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
The clinical trial data demonstrate that the infection risk with remibrutinib is comparable to
placebo with no evidence of opportunistic/atypical infections or increase in frequency or severity
of infections attributable to the drug. There was no change to the risk of infection with long-term
use up to 1 year (52 weeks).
Conclusion
Despite its mechanism of action and known BTKi class effects for increasing susceptibility to
infection, remibrutinib 25 mg BID does not appear to increase infection risk in subjects with
CSU.

7.7.3. Cytopenias
Issue
Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to cause cytopenias.
Background
BTK inhibitors are associated with an increased risk of cytopenias due to the pharmacological
effect on B-cell signaling. BTK inhibitors, particularly less selective agents, may also have offtarget effects on hematopoietic progenitors. The incidence and severity of these cytopenias vary
by agent and clinical context, such as concomitant use with other cytotoxic agents or
chemoimmunotherapy.
Assessment
Cytopenia was captured as an AESI for remibrutinib and reported using the standardized
MedDRA query ‘Haematopoietic cytopenias (broad).’ The evaluation focused on monitoring for
decreases in blood cell counts, particularly neutrophils, lymphocytes, and platelets, and assessing
any potential clinical consequences such as increased infection risk or bleeding events. During
the 24-week placebo-controlled period in the pivotal trials, cytopenia AESIs occurred
infrequently in both the remibrutinib 25-mg BID group (22, 3.6%) and the placebo group (6,
2.0%) as shown in Table 27.

Abbreviations: AESI, adverse event of special interest; CI, confidence interval; MedDRA, Medical Dictionary for Regulatory

SMQ, standardized MedDRA query

All cytopenia AESIs were mild (75.0% versus 100.0%) or moderate (25.0% versus 0%) in
severity, with no severe events reported. There were no SAEs related to cytopenia. The median
time-to-event onset was 70.0 days in the remibrutinib group versus 35.0 days in the placebo
group, with median event durations of 26.0 and 28.0 days, respectively. Most events resolved
(90.6% versus 88.9%) while continuing the drug, and only 31.3% of events in the remibrutinib
group were considered related to study treatment. No action was taken with the study treatment
for most events (84.4%), with study treatment interrupted for only three events (9.4%) and
discontinued for one event (3.1%) in the remibrutinib group. The difference between treatment
groups was primarily driven by TEAEs for neutropenia (1.2% versus 0.3%) and neutrophil count
decreased (0.3% versus 0%) in the remibrutinib and placebo groups, respectively. Grade 3
neutropenia, defined as an absolute neutrophil count of 500 to <1000/mm³, occurred in two
subjects in the remibrutinib group compared to one subject in the placebo group, neither of
which was associated with a clinical infection. One occurred in a 42-year-old female at Week 16
who remained on treatment. She recovered by Week 20 but then worsened to Grade 1 (absolute
neutrophil count between lower limit of normal and 1500/mm³). The second occurred in a 69year-old female at Week 8 who, after interrupting treatment for 8 days, improved to Grade 1 at
Week 12.
During the placebo-controlled period, thrombocytopenia was reported in 3 patients (0.5%) in the
remibrutinib treatment arm compared to 1 patient (0.3%) in the placebo group. All reported
thrombocytopenia events were mild or moderate in severity, with no severe cases documented.
One notable case involved a 21-year-old male patient who had a Grade 1 low platelet count at
baseline and Week 2 that initially recovered to normal levels, but then developed a Grade 3 low
platelet count of 39 × 10⁹/L at Week 24. Importantly, this patient's platelet count showed normal
values when retested just 2 days later, suggesting a transient laboratory finding rather than
sustained thrombocytopenia. Another case involved a 29-year-old female patient who was
initially reported to have a Grade 4 platelet decrease, but this was later determined to be due to
incorrect reporting of laboratory parameter units by the local laboratory, with no clinically
relevant platelet decrease actually occurring. There was no temporal association between low
platelet counts and bleeding AESIs in either treatment group. The infrequent low platelet counts,
and increased prothrombin INR values were not temporally associated with bleeding events, and
the analysis revealed no correlation between bleeding AESIs and notable decreases in platelet
counts.
During the 52-week study periods, the EAOR did not increase (8.9 per 100 patient-years)
compared to the placebo-controlled period (12.1 per 100 patient-years), suggesting no increase in
cytopenia AESIs with longer treatment duration (see Section 17.1 for additional details).

74
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
In addition to cytopenia AESIs, laboratory data were also analyzed because not all lab
abnormalities were captured as TEAEs by investigators. Laboratory analysis revealed minimal
shifts in blood cell counts, with a downward shift in median neutrophil counts (from 4.00x10⁹
cells/L at baseline to 3.75x10⁹ cells/L at Week 2) and platelet counts (from 272x10⁹ cells/L to
243x10⁹ cells/L at Week 2) observed in the remibrutinib group. However, these levels remained
stable throughout treatment and within normal ranges, returning to baseline levels upon
treatment cessation. Grade 3 and Grade 4 hematology abnormalities were infrequent and
generally balanced between treatment groups, with no additional events identified beyond those
that were captured as AESIs. There was no association between cytopenias and clinical bleeding
events or infections.
Conclusion
Cytopenia occurred infrequently in the trial with small numerical differences between treatment
groups. Although remibrutinib-treated subjects were more likely to report neutropenia or
thrombocytopenia, the observed imbalances do not appear clinically significant.

7.7.4. Vaccine Immune Response
Issue
Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib may impact the immune response to
vaccinations.

(b) (4)

Background
The mechanism of action of BTK inhibitors, blockage of B-cell receptor and myeloid fragment
crystallizable receptor mediated signaling with resultant decreased B-cell activation, antibody
class-switching, expansion, and cytokine production would be expected to impact the
immunogenicity of vaccines. By decreasing antigen presentation to T-cells, BTK inhibitors may
lead to altered T-cell responses and interferon induction by vaccination. To evaluate the impact
of remibrutinib on vaccine response, the Applicant conducted an immunogenicity study in
healthy adults. In consultation with CBER, the Agency provided feedback on the study (written
responses dated July 19, 2022) outlining key concerns such as whether a 1-week interruption of
remibrutinib would be sufficient for immune system recovery, the appropriateness of conducting
the study in healthy volunteers rather than the intended subject population, and the selection of
immunogenicity endpoints. The Agency emphasized that immunogenicity studies are inherently
(b) (4) , cautioning that vaccine responses
difficult to interpret
cannot serve as surrogates for overall immune competence.
Assessment
Study F12101 was a double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study to evaluate the
impact of concomitant or interrupted remibrutinib treatment regimens in comparison with
placebo on the response to three vaccines in healthy subjects. A total of 107 subjects were
randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to one of the three treatment arms: concomitant remibrutinib
treatment, interrupted remibrutinib treatment, or placebo. Subjects were administered three
different vaccines: a T-cell-dependent vaccine (quadrivalent seasonal influenza [Influsplit
Tetra/Fluarix Tetra, GSK]), a T-cell-independent 23-valent pneumococcal polysaccharide
75
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
vaccine (PPV-23, Pneumovax 23), and a T-cell-dependent neoantigen (keyhole Limpet
hemocyanin [KLH]), on Day 15. See Section 15.6 for a detailed description of the study design.
In consultation with CBER/Office of Vaccines Research and Review, the review team concluded
(b) (4)
that the results of the vaccine immune response study were not relevant
for various reasons. Primarily, the study failed to employ the immunogenicity evaluations that
CBER had previously accepted for determining vaccine effectiveness. Specifically, it did not
measure geometric mean titers and proper seroconversion rates for influenza vaccines, nor did it
use the opsonophagocytic antibody assay for pneumococcal vaccines, despite CBER providing
guidance on these requirements in the 2022 Type C meeting responses to the Applicant. Instead,
the study used less appropriate endpoints including IgG antibody levels for pneumococcal
(b) (4) .
assessment, which are insufficient
The inclusion of KLH as a study component further limited the study's regulatory relevance.
CBER noted that KLH is not an FDA-licensed vaccine and that antibody responses to KLH lack
established clinical significance for predicting responses to licensed vaccines. Finally, the study
was conducted in healthy volunteers rather than the intended CSU patient population, limiting
extrapolation of results.
Regarding avoidance of live or live-attenuated vaccines, with input from CBER, the review team
agreed that such recommendations would be appropriate for inclusion in Sections 5 (Warnings
and Precautions) and 17 (Patient Counseling Information) of the USPI. Although not routinely
included in labeling for other BTKi, recommendations to avoid live/live-attenuated vaccines
have similarly been included in labeling for other immunomodulators (in the absence of clinical
data), and patients with CSU are more likely to be candidates for live virus vaccines compared to
the immunocompromised oncology populations typically treated with approved BTK inhibitors.
For more details, refer to the CBER consult memo (Inter-Center Consult No.: 01066955) dated
June 2, 2025, in the Document Archiving, Reporting, and Regulatory Tracking System
(DARRTS) (Reference ID 5601342).
Conclusion
(b) (4)

Given the lack of clinical data on the effects of live or live-attenuated vaccines in subjects
receiving remibrutinib, the review team concurs with adding a Warning and Precaution to avoid
use of live and live-attenuated vaccines.

7.7.5. Use in Subjects With Hepatic Impairment
Issue
(b) (4)

patients with hepatic impairment

(HI).
Background
Unlike drugs that can be dose-adjusted to accommodate patient-specific factors or drug
interactions, the therapeutic window and safety profile of remibrutinib do not permit dose
reductions or increases to compensate for altered drug exposure as only a single dose of
remibrutinib (i.e., 25 mg BID) was evaluated in the pivotal phase 3 trials A2301 and A2302. As
76
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
a result, there are limited clinical data to guide dose adjustments for patients with HI who
experience 2- to 3.5-fold increases in drug exposure across all degrees of liver dysfunction (mild,
moderate, and severe).
Assessment
The Applicant conducted a dedicated HI study, Study A2101, in which the pharmacokinetics of
remibrutinib following administration of 25 mg BID was evaluated in subjects with mild (ChildPugh Class A), moderate (Child-Pugh Class B), and severe (Child-Pugh Class C) HI compared to
subjects with normal hepatic function matched for gender, age, and body weight. Following the
administration of 25-mg BID remibrutinib, the geometric mean ratios for subjects with mild,
moderate, and severe HI compared to subjects with normal hepatic function were 1.85, 1.65, and
1.99 for maximum plasma concentration (Cmax), respectively, and 2.33, 2.3, and 3.49 for
AUClast,ss, respectively (Figure 10, Figure 35). For additional details, refer to Section 14.2.5.
Figure 10. Box Plots of Cmax and AUCtau in Subjects With Mild, Moderate, and Severe Hepatic
Impairment vs. Healthy Controls

Source: Reviewer’s Analysis based on ADPP.xpt dataset for Study A2101
Abbreviations: AUCtau, area under the concentration-time curve over the dosing interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration;
HI, hepatic impairment
(b) (4)

(b) (4)

However, safety data
from healthy subjects is not considered appropriate to reflect the safety in subjects with CSU. In
addition, the safety data in subjects with CSU derived from Studies A2201 and A2201E1 are
limited by the small number of subjects exposed for a short duration in the controlled Study
A2201 and the inherent limitations of uncontrolled, unblinded data in Study A2201E1. Thus,
exposure-response analyses for safety are not adequate to determine that chronic exposure to
higher doses/exposures of remibrutinib is safe.
Furthermore, CSU is not a life-threatening disease, and other approved therapies are available for
the treatment of CSU, including in patients with HI. Given the potential risks associated with
remibrutinib (e.g., bleeding events) and the lack of robust safety data with remibrutinib
exposures 2.3- to 3.5-fold greater than that of the recommended dosage, the benefit-risk of
exposing CSU patients with HI to higher exposures of remibrutinib is not favorable.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
Conclusion
Given the limitations in assessing safety following administration of higher doses of
remibrutinib, the use of remibrutinib in subjects with any degree of HI (mild, moderate, or
severe) should be avoided. Consideration of the condition and benefit/risk assessment led to
conservative dose-adjustment recommendations, favoring contraindications rather than dose
modifications when adequate safety data were unavailable to support exposure changes.

7.7.6. Concomitant Use With CYP3A4 Modulators
Issue
(b) (4)

concomitant use of strong or

moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors.
Background
Unlike drugs that can be dose-adjusted to accommodate patient-specific factors or drug
interactions, the therapeutic window and safety profile of remibrutinib do not permit dose
reductions or increases to compensate for altered drug exposure as only a single dose of
remibrutinib (i.e., 25 mg BID) was evaluated in the pivotal phase 3 Trials A2301 and A2302.
The drug's susceptibility to CYP3A4-mediated interactions creates scenarios where dose
adjustments would theoretically be beneficial but are not available as management strategies
because there are limited clinical data to guide dose adjustments.
Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors increase remibrutinib exposure by 3- to 4-fold, while strong inducers
decrease exposure by 74% to 78%, yet there are no established dose-reduction or increase
protocols to maintain therapeutic levels during these interactions.
Assessment
Strong CYP3A4 Inhibition
Study X2103 evaluated the impact of multiple oral doses of ritonavir 100 mg, a strong
CYP3A4/P-glycoprotein (P-gp) inhibitor, or grapefruit juice, an intestinal CYP3A4 inhibitor, on
the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib following administration of 20-mg remibrutinib capsules
once daily in healthy subjects (see Section 14.2.6.1). Initially, remibrutinib 50 mg once daily was
administered before evaluating the drug-drug interactions (DDIs) with ritonavir and grapefruit
juice in order to achieve near-complete BTK receptor occupancy and steady-state conditions,
thereby minimizing the influence of target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) on remibrutinib
PK measurements. Given the short apparent half-life of remibrutinib, it is not expected that
administration of higher doses (i.e., 50 mg) will influence the pharmacokinetics following
administration of the 20-mg dose used to evaluate the impact of CYP3A4 inhibition. The
coadministration of ritonavir with remibrutinib 20 mg resulted in a 3.32-, 4.5-, and 4.31-fold
increase in Cmax, AUClast and AUC0-inf of remibrutinib, respectively (see Section 14.2.6.1).
The coadministration of grapefruit juice with remibrutinib 20 mg resulted in a 1.24-, 1.33- and
1.28-fold increase in Cmax, AUClast and AUC0-inf of remibrutinib, respectively, indicating a
minimal intestinal first pass-effect on remibrutinib.
78
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
Physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) simulations predict a 2.67- and 3.65-fold
increase in Cmax,ss and AUCtau, respectively, of remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral
administration of ritonavir (100 mg BID for 7 days) in healthy subjects. Additional PBPK
simulations with other strong CYP3A4 inhibitors predict a 2.47- and 3.21-fold increase in Cmax,ss
and AUCtau of remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral administration of itraconazole (200 mg
QD for 7 days), and a 2.53- and 3.38-fold increase in Cmax,ss and AUCtau of remibrutinib 25 mg
BID following the oral administration of ketoconazole (200 mg BID for 7 days) in healthy
subjects (see Section 14.5.3).
Moderate CYP3A4 Inhibition
PBPK simulations predict a 1.9- and 2.27-fold increase in Cmax,ss and AUCtau, respectively, of
remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral administration of erythromycin (500 mg QD for 7
days) in healthy subjects. Fluconazole, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, administered at 200 mg
QD for 7 days was predicted to increase the Cmax,ss and AUCtau of remibrutinib 25 mg BID by
1.67- and 1.89-fold, respectively (see Section 14.5.3).
Weak CYP3A4 Inhibition
PBPK simulations predict a 1.04-fold increase in both Cmax,ss and AUCtau of remibrutinib 25 mg
BID following the oral administration of fluvoxamine (50 mg QD for 7 days) in healthy subjects.
CYP3A4 Inducers
Study A02103 investigated the impact of carbamazepine (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) at a dosage
of 300 mg BID on the exposure to remibrutinib 100 mg BID. Carbamazepine significantly
decreased the Cmax,ss, AUCtau, and AUClast,ss of remibrutinib 100 mg by 74%, 77%, and 78%,
respectively. Refer to Section 14.2.6.2 for additional details.
PBPK simulations predict a 64% and 74% reduction in the Cmax and AUCtau, respectively, of
remibrutinib 25 BID when coadministered with carbamazepine compared to administration of
remibrutinib alone. In addition, rifampicin (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) administered at 600 mg
QD was predicted to decrease the Cmax,ss and AUCtau of remibrutinib 25 mg BID by 88% and
91%, respectively, compared to administration of remibrutinib alone (see Section 14.5.3).
Efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) administered at 600 mg QD for 7 days was predicted to
decrease the Cmax,ss and AUCtau of remibrutinib 25 BID by 60% and 64%, respectively (see
Section 14.5.3). Dexamethasone (a weak CYP3A4 inducer) administered at 8 mg BID for 7 days
was predicted to decrease the Cmax,ss and AUCtau of remibrutinib 25 BID by 33% and 38%,
respectively (see Section 14.5.3).
Overall, the data indicate that strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors significantly increase the
(b) (4)
exposure to remibrutinib 25 mg BID.

(b) (4)

the benefit-risk of exposing patients with CSU to higher exposures of
remibrutinib following coadministration with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors is not
favorable.
(b) (4)

79
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
Conclusion
The use of remibrutinib with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors should be avoided, and the
USPI will be revised accordingly. Given the large decreases in remibrutinib exposure and the
potential for loss of efficacy, coadministration of remibrutinib with moderate or strong CYP3A
inducers should be avoided.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
The dose-adjustment recommendations for remibrutinib are based on a dataset encompassing
population pharmacokinetic analyses, dedicated special population studies, and drug-interaction
trials across diverse patient populations. Population pharmacokinetic modeling was conducted
using data from 1152 subjects across multiple clinical trials, providing the foundation for
evaluating intrinsic factors including age (18 to 80 years), sex (63.5% females, 36.5% males),
body weight (39 to 162 kg), and race/ethnicity distributions. For renal impairment evaluation, the
dataset comprised 904 subjects (78.5%) with normal renal function (creatinine clearance
≥90 mL/min), 222 subjects (19.3%) with mild impairment (60 to 90 mL/min), 25 subjects (2.2%)
with mild-to-moderate impairment (45 to 60 mL/min), and one subject with severe impairment
(15 to 30 mL/min), supplemented by mass balance Study X2104 data showing minimal renal
elimination of unchanged drug (0.65% of oral dose).
Hepatic impairment assessment relied on dedicated Study A2101, which evaluated remibrutinib
25 mg BID in subjects with mild (Child-Pugh Class A), moderate (Child-Pugh Class B), and
severe (Child-Pugh Class C) hepatic impairment compared to matched healthy controls. Druginteraction data were derived from multiple clinical studies, including Study X2103 evaluating
ritonavir (strong CYP3A4 inhibitor) and grapefruit juice effects, Study A02103 assessing
carbamazepine (strong CYP3A4 inducer) impact, and Study X2102 examining effects on
CYP3A4, CYP2C9, and CYP1A2 substrates using probe drugs midazolam, tolbutamide, and
caffeine. Additional interaction studies evaluated P-gp and breast cancer resistance protein
(BCRP) substrate effects using digoxin and rosuvastatin as probe drugs. Food-effect data came
from Study A02104, while acid-reducing agent interactions were assessed through pooled
analysis of concomitant medication use in Trials A2201, A2301, and A2302.
The safety database supporting dose-adjustment decisions included exposure-response analyses
from three safety pools: E-R Safety Pool 1a (24-week placebo-controlled data from Trials A2301
and A2302), E-R Safety Pool 1b (entire treatment period from Trials A2301 and A2302), and ER Safety Pool 2 (12-week data from Trials A2201, A2301, and A2302). However, the adequacy
of safety data for higher exposures was limited, with the review team noting insufficient data
from healthy subjects receiving up to 600 mg once daily and limited controlled data from
subjects with CSU receiving 100 mg BID in Studies A2201 and A2201E1. Thus, exposureresponse analyses for safety are not appropriate to inform on safety following chronic exposure
to higher doses/exposures of remibrutinib. PBPK modeling supplemented clinical data to predict
interactions with various CYP3A4 modulators not directly studied, providing additional support
for drug-interaction recommendations.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)

8.1. Intrinsic Factors
8.1.1. Age
No dosage adjustment is required based on age.
Population pharmacokinetic (popPK) analyses indicated that age (range 18 to 80 years) does not
significantly impact the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib in subjects with CSU.

8.1.2. Body Weight
No dosage adjustment is required based on body weight.
PopPK modeling demonstrated a strong positive correlation between fat-free mass (FFM) and fat
mass (FM) with body weight. The final population PK model indicated that FFM and FM were
significant covariates affecting remibrutinib clearance, while only FM was a significant covariate
affecting volume of distribution. An increase in FM is expected to elevate the exposure to
remibrutinib, which is moderately lipophilic. Given that body weight is composed of both FFM
and FM, the impact of body weight on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was assessed
through the combined effects of FFM and FM. This analysis estimates a 20% increase in area
under the AUC for subjects with CSU with a total body weight of 49 kg (5th percentile of the
overall dataset) and a 20% decrease in AUC for subjects with CSU with a total body weight of
109 kg (95th percentile of the overall dataset) relative to a typical patient with body weight of
70 kg. Overall, the impact of body weight (range 39 to 162 kg) on remibrutinib exposure is not
considered clinically relevant.

8.1.3. Sex
No dosage adjustment is required based on sex.
The population PK results indicated a disparity in the distributions of FFM and FM between
males and females, primarily due to the lower FFM and higher FM in females compared to males
(Figure 11). After accounting for body weight, there was no notable effect of sex (63.5% females
and 36.5% males) on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib in subjects with CSU.

Reference ID: 5668341

8.1.4. Race/Ethnicity
No dosage adjustment is required based on race/ethnicity.
The observed exposure to remibrutinib by race measured 45 minutes following the
administration of remibrutinib at 25 mg BID at Week 12 in phase 3 Trials A2301 and A2302
combined is provided in Figure 12. The postdose concentration of remibrutinib is comparable
between the White (54.2%, N=265), Black (2.8%, N=14), Chinese (12.6%, N=62), and Asian
non-Chinese (i.e., Indian (13.3%, N=65), Vietnamese (1.6%, N=8), and Korean (4%, N=20))
populations. Although there was a higher median concentration observed in the Japanese
population (3.8%, N=19), it is within the range of variability of PK observations in the other
populations. In addition, the proportion of the observed dataset that fell within the Japanese
population was relatively small as compared to other race/ethnicity groups. Thus, the finding of
greater median exposure among Japanese subjects should be interpreted with caution.
The overall dataset for popPK analysis was comprised of 59.3% (N=683) non-Asian (i.e., White,
Black, and Others) subjects, 8.8% (N=101) Mainland Chinese subjects, 12.2% (N=141) Japanese
subjects, and 19.7% (N=227) other Asian (i.e., East Asian, no Mainland Chinese, Japanese; and
Asian, no East Asian) subjects. In popPK analysis, the race/ethnicity groups of Mainland
Chinese, Japanese, and Other Asian were identified as significant covariates affecting the
clearance of remibrutinib. All race/ethnicity groups were determined to have lower clearance
relative to the reference population of non-Asian. Consequently, popPK simulations indicate that
remibrutinib AUC increases by 21% in Mainland Chinese subjects, 20% in Japanese subjects,
and 30% in Other Asian subjects compared to non-Asian subjects, all at the same body weight of
70 kg (Figure 13).
Overall, these differences in exposure are not considered to be clinically relevant.

8.1.5. Renal Impairment
No dosage adjustment is required for subjects with renal impairment.
The renal clearance of remibrutinib was determined in mass balance Study X2104 in which
healthy subjects received oral or intravenous (IV) doses of radiolabeled remibrutinib. Both oral
and IV administration were evaluated as there was incomplete recovery of radioactivity in the
oral arm due to technical issues. For additional details, refer to Section 14.2.2. Following oral
administration, about 24% of the dose was recovered in the urine. The amount of unchanged
remibrutinib excreted in urine was minimal, accounting for 0.65% of the oral dose, while the
most abundant metabolite excreted in urine accounted for about 3.5% of the oral dose. Following
IV dosing, about 26 to 30% of the dose was recovered in the urine. The percentage of unchanged
remibrutinib excreted in urine was 2.93%. These findings suggest that renal excretion is a minor
elimination pathway for remibrutinib. As additional supportive evidence in Study X2101 the
renal clearance of remibrutinib following administration of multiple doses of 100 or 200 mg BID
was found to constitute <1% of the total blood clearance (refer to Section 14.2.1.1).
PopPK analysis did not identify creatinine clearance (CrCL; range 23 to 275 mL/min) as a
significant covariate affecting the clearance of remibrutinib (Figure 14). The data represent 904
subjects (78.5%) with normal renal function (CrCL ≥90 mL/min), 222 subjects (19.3%) subjects
with mild renal impairment (CrCL 60 to 90 mL/min); 25 subjects (2.2%) with mild-to-moderate
renal impairment (CrCL 45 to 60 mL/min); and 1 subject (0.1%) with severe renal impairment
(CrCL 15 to 30 mL/min). Based on the pharmacokinetic data from Studies X2104 and X2101, as
well as the popPK analysis, a dedicated renal impairment study was not considered necessary.
Therefore, no dose adjustment is required for CSU subjects with renal impairment.

Clearance in Pooled Data, Log Scale

8.1.6. Hepatic Impairment
The use of remibrutinib should be avoided in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic
impairment. Refer to key safety review issue in Section 7.7.5 for details.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
8.2.1. Food Effect
Remibrutinib can be administered with or without food.
The effect of food on exposure to remibrutinib was assessed in healthy subjects in Study A02104
following administration of 25 mg BID in the intended to-be-marketed FCT formulation. For
additional details, refer to Section 14.2.4. Notably, the food effect was evaluated at steady state.
However, the goal of food-effect evaluation is to assess the food effect on drug absorption. The
about 2-fold accumulation of remibrutinib systemic exposure at steady state is not contributed by
drug absorption. Therefore, the food effect on remibrutinib at steady state can be extrapolated to
single dose, given the short half-life of remibrutinib (1 to 2 hours).
When remibrutinib FCTs at a dosage of 25 mg BID were coadministered with a high-fat, highcalorie meal (1000 calories, 50% fat), the pharmacokinetic parameters were not significantly
altered (see Section 14.2.4). Specifically, the AUC0-12h increased by approximately 33%, while
Cmax decreased by roughly 5%. Additionally, the Tmax was delayed by 1 hour. These differences
in exposure are not considered to be clinically meaningful. Therefore, remibrutinib may be
administered with or without food.

8.2.2. Effect of Remibrutinib on Other Drugs
The potential inhibition or induction of CYP enzymes and transporters by remibrutinib was
investigated through in vitro and in vivo DDI studies. Notably, in in vivo studies, the effects of
remibrutinib on the pharmacokinetics of substrates for CYP3A4, CYP2C9, CYP1A2, P-gp,
BCRP, and organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP)1B1 were studied at a higher dosage
(i.e., 100 mg BID) than the recommended 25-mg BID dosage. PBPK simulations were
conducted to estimate the clinical DDI risk of remibrutinib at the recommended dosage of 25 mg
BID. The review team concluded that the findings from the studies conducted at the higher
dosage of remibrutinib are applicable to the recommended 25-mg BID dosage.
Based on in vitro studies and static drug-interaction potential assessments, remibrutinib is not
expected to significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of concomitant drugs that are substrates of
CYP enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A) or
transporters (bile salt export pump [BSEP], OATP1B1, OATP1B3, organic cation transporter
[OCT]1, OCT2, organic anion transporter [OAT]1, OAT3, multidrug and toxin extrusion protein
[MATE]1, MATE2-K, and sodium taurocholate cotransporting polypeptide), with the exception
of P-gp and BCRP (Table 28).

85
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
CYP3A4 Substrates
In vitro studies have demonstrated that remibrutinib acts as a reversible inhibitor (unbound
inhibition constant [reversible inhibition] [Ki,u] =14.1µM), time-dependent inhibitor (unbound
inhibition constant [time-dependent inhibition] [KI,u] =6.9µM) and inducer (Emax =28.1 and halfmaximal effective concentration [EC50]=5.08µM) of CYP3A4. Consequently, the net effect of
remibrutinib on CYP3A4 function may exhibit time-dependent characteristics. The observed
reversible inhibition effect is likely more pronounced at the initiation of treatment, while the
induction effect may be most evident at steady state, following administration of multiple doses
of remibrutinib.
Although there is low potential for remibrutinib to have a clinically relevant impact on the
pharmacokinetics of CYP3A substrates based on static DDI assessment (Table 28), the potential
of remibrutinib to inhibit or induce CYP3A4 enzymes was evaluated following administration of
multiple oral doses of remibrutinib 100-mg capsules in healthy female subjects using midazolam
(administered on Day 3 and Day 11, following the initiation of remibrutinib dosing) as the probe
substrate for CYP3A4 (Study X2102, see Section 14.2.6.3). When midazolam was
coadministered with multiple doses of remibrutinib 100 mg on Day 3, the plasma concentrations
of midazolam were not clinically meaningfully affected, with 36% increase in Cmax and 45%
increase in AUC. Similar results were observed on Day 11, with less than 27% increase in
midazolam Cmax and 43% increase in midazolam AUC. Based on the observed effects of
remibrutinib administered at four times the recommended dosage on midazolam exposure on
Days 3 and 11, it can be inferred that remibrutinib has a low potential to inhibit or induce
CYP3A4 substrates at the recommended remibrutinib dosage of 25 mg BID.
PBPK simulations did not predict a clinically relevant DDI effect on the exposure to the sensitive
CYP3A substrate midazolam following coadministration with remibrutinib at the recommended
dosage of 25 mg BID on Days 3 and 11. PBPK simulations estimate an 18% increase in
midazolam Cmax and 20% increase in midazolam AUC on Day 3; and a 22% increase in Cmax and
25% increase in AUC on Day 11 (see Section 14.5.2).
To further assess the potential of remibrutinib to affect the pharmacokinetics of CYP3A4
substrates, Study X2102 also evaluated the DDI potential between multiple oral doses of
remibrutinib 100-mg capsules and combined oral contraceptives (OCs), containing
ethinylestradiol (EE) and levonorgestrel (LVG), administered on Day 15 following the initiation
of remibrutinib dosing. Multiple doses of remibrutinib 100 mg BID did not significantly impact
the exposure to EE and LVG, with 28% increase in the Cmax and 18% increase in the AUC of EE
and 36% increase in the Cmax and 39% increase in the AUC of LVG (see Section 14.2.6.3).
PBPK simulations did not predict a clinically relevant DDI effect on EE pharmacokinetics
following coadministration with remibrutinib 25 mg BID (7% increase in Cmax and 8% increase
in AUC) (see Section 14.5.3).
Taken together, remibrutinib has low potential to inhibit or induce CYP3A in vivo. Sensitive
CYP3A substrates or combined OCs containing EE and LVG may be coadministered with
remibrutinib. No clinically relevant impact on the efficacy of oral contraceptives is expected
following coadministration with remibrutinib.

86
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
CYP2C9 Substrates
In vitro studies have demonstrated that remibrutinib acts as a reversible inhibitor (Ki,u =7.7µM)
and inducer (Emax =3.02 and EC50=5.1µM) of CYP2C9. However, based on static DDI
assessments, remibrutinib is not expected to have a clinically relevant impact on the
pharmacokinetics of CYP2C9 substrates in vivo (Table 28). Despite this, the potential for
multiple oral doses of remibrutinib 100 mg BID to inhibit or induce CYP2C9 was evaluated in
healthy female subjects using tolbutamide as the probe substrate (Study X2102, see
Section 14.2.6.3). Tolbutamide was administered on Day 3 and Day 11 following the initiation of
remibrutinib treatment. Results demonstrated a 27% increase in tolbutamide plasma
concentration measured at 24 hours postdose (C24h) on Day 3 and a 30% increase in
tolbutamide C24h on Day 11 following coadministration with remibrutinib 100 mg BID. The
Applicant selected tolbutamide C24h as a metric for CYP2C9 activity due to its purported strong
correlation with tolbutamide clearance (Jetter et al. 2004). However, based on the findings from
Jetter et al. (2004), tolbutamide C24h may overestimate the magnitude of drug interaction
between remibrutinib and tolbutamide compared to that determined using Cmax and AUC. As
multiple oral doses of remibrutinib 100 mg yielded an approximate 30% increase in tolbutamide
C24h, a lower magnitude may have been observed based on Cmax and AUC. Hence, remibrutinib
100 mg is unlikely to have clinically relevant impact on the exposure to CYP2C9 substrates.
The outcomes of a static DDI model, which indicated no significant clinical interaction risk with
CYP2C9 substrates, and the absence of a complete pharmacokinetic profile for tolbutamide,
obviate the necessity for PBPK simulations for the impact of remibrutinib 25 mg BID on
CYP2C9 substrates.
CYP1A2 Substrates
In vitro studies have demonstrated that remibrutinib acts as a reversible inhibitor (Ki,u =35.3µM)
and inducer (Emax =5.47, EC50=4.55µM) of CYP1A2. However, based on static DDI assessments,
remibrutinib is not expected to have a clinically relevant impact on the pharmacokinetics of
CYP1A2 substrates in vivo. The potential for multiple oral doses of remibrutinib 100 mg BID to
inhibit or induce CYP1A2 was evaluated in healthy female subjects using caffeine as a probe
substrate (Study X2102, see Section 14.2.6.3). Caffeine was administered on Day 3 and Day 11
following the initiation of remibrutinib treatment. The administration of multiple doses of
remibrutinib 100 mg BID did not affect caffeine plasma concentration measured at 6 hours
postdose (C6h). In addition, there was little change in the paraxanthine to caffeine ratio at 6
hours, with reductions of 3% and 12% on Day 3 and Day 11, respectively.
The outcomes of a static DDI model, which indicated no significant clinical interaction risk with
CYP1A2 substrates, and the absence of a complete pharmacokinetic profile for caffeine, obviate
the necessity for PBPK simulations to assess the impact of remibrutinib 25 mg BID.
P-gp Substrates
In vitro, remibrutinib inhibited intestinal P-gp (inhibition constant [reversible inhibition]
[Ki] =0.382µM) and static DDI assessments predict a potential impact of remibrutinib on P-gp
substrates with R-value =517. The impact of remibrutinib on the pharmacokinetics of digoxin, a
P-gp substrate, was investigated in healthy subjects in Study A02103 (see Section 14.2.6.2). In
healthy subjects, the Cmax and AUClast of digoxin increased by 108% and 45%, respectively,
87
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
following coadministration with remibrutinib 100 mg BID (see Section 14.2.6.2). PBPK
(b) (4) in the C
simulations predicted
max and AUC, respectively, of digoxin when
coadministered with multiple oral doses of remibrutinib 100 mg BID. The PBPK simulations
tend to mis-predict the observed magnitude of the interaction between remibrutinib 100 mg BID
and digoxin (see Section 14.5.3). Therefore, due to the inherent limitations of model predictions
and confidence in the predicted results, the clinical findings from Study A02103, conducted with
the 100-mg BID dosage of remibrutinib, should be applicable to subjects receiving the lower
dosage of 25 mg BID. The magnitude of effect at a remibrutinib dosage of 25 mg BID is
expected to be lower than that at 100 mg BID. However, the difference in effect is unclear
without the conduct of a dedicated clinical study.
It is therefore recommended to monitor more frequently for adverse reactions when remibrutinib
is administered with P-gp substrates that have a narrow therapeutic index (i.e., drugs for which
minimal concentration changes can lead to clinically significant differences in efficacy or
safety).
BCRP/OATP1B1 Substrates
In vitro, remibrutinib inhibited intestinal BCRP (Ki =0.467µM) and static DDI assessments
predicted a potential impact of remibrutinib on BCRP substrates with R-value =423. Although
remibrutinib inhibited OATP1B1 (Ki,u =2.36µM) and OATP1B3 (Ki,u =7.55µM) in vitro, static
DDI assessment predicted no drug-interaction potential between remibrutinib and OATP1B1/3
substrates (e.g., rosuvastatin). The impact of remibrutinib on the pharmacokinetics of
rosuvastatin, a BCRP and OATP1B substrate, was investigated in healthy subjects in Study
A02103. In Study A02103, the Cmax and AUClast of rosuvastatin increased by 56% and 65%,
respectively, following coadministration with remibrutinib 100 mg BID. To distinguish between
inhibition of BCRP and OATP1B, changes in coproporphyrin I exposure were also assessed. As
there was no change in the plasma exposure to coproporphyrin I, it is expected that inhibition of
OATP1B by remibrutinib is negligible (see Section 14.2.6.2). Given that in vitro data predict a
low risk of inhibition of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 at clinically relevant concentrations, the
increase in rosuvastatin plasma exposure can be attributed to an inhibitory effect of remibrutinib
on BCRP.
PBPK simulations tended to mis-predict the observed magnitude of the interaction between
remibrutinib 100 mg BID and rosuvastatin (see Section 14.5.3). Therefore, due to the inherent
limitations of model predictions and confidence in the predicted results, the clinical findings
from Study A02103, conducted with the 100 mg BID dosage of remibrutinib, should be
applicable to subjects receiving the lower dosage of 25 mg BID. The magnitude of effect at a
remibrutinib dosage of 25 mg BID is expected to be lower than that at 100 mg BID. However,
the difference in effect is unclear without conduct of a dedicated clinical study.
It is therefore recommended to monitor more frequently for adverse reactions when remibrutinib
is administered with BCRP substrates that have a narrow therapeutic index (i.e., drugs for which
minimal concentration changes can lead to clinically significant differences in efficacy or
safety).

Antithrombotic Agents
The risks and benefits of coadministration of antithrombotic agents with remibrutinib should be
considered due to BTK inhibitor drug class effect which inhibits the platelet activation. Refer to
safety review issue in Section 7.7.1.

8.2.3. Effect of Other Drugs on Remibrutinib
In vitro data indicate that metabolism of remibrutinib is predominantly driven by CYP3A4 with a
minor contribution from CYP2C19. In vitro studies also determined that remibrutinib is a
substrate for P-gp, but not for other efflux and uptake transporters. For additional details, refer to
Section 14.1.
The effect of other drugs on remibrutinib as an object was evaluated in two clinical DDI studies,
X2103 and A02103. In Study X2103, the impact of ritonavir (CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitor) and
grapefruit juice (intestinal CYP3A4 inhibitor) were evaluated. In Study A02103, the impact of
carbamazepine (CYP3A4 inducer) was assessed. For additional details, refer to Section 14.2.6.1
and Section 14.2.6.2. PBPK modeling and simulation was also used to predict the impact of
moderate and strong CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib
25 mg BID.
CYP3A Induction or Inhibition
Use of remibrutinib with moderate or strong CYP3A4 inducers and inhibitors should be avoided.
Refer to safety review issue in Section 7.7.6.
P-gp Inhibition
In vitro studies suggest that remibrutinib exhibits high passive permeability at concentrations of
50µM and above, while at lower concentrations (e.g., 0.5µM), its permeability is reduced due to
90
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5668341

NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
an apically directed P-gp-mediated efflux process. When remibrutinib is administered at 25 mg,
the intestinal local concentration of remibrutinib is expected to be approximately 197µM,
suggesting a high passive permeability at the recommended dose of 25 mg twice daily.
Based on clinical human absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) data,
unchanged remibrutinib was not detected in feces following intravenous administration.
Following oral administration, the amount of unchanged remibrutinib in feces was 1.21% of the
oral dose. Therefore, DDI effects mediated via P-gp inhibition are not expected.
Acid-Reducing Agents
No clinically relevant changes in remibrutinib exposure are expected when remibrutinib is
coadministered with acid-reducing agents (ARAs).
Remibrutinib is a weak base with pH-dependent solubility and, thus, remibrutinib absorption
may be reduced at higher pH (e.g., coadministration of ARAs). The proportion of subjects who
coadministered remibrutinib with proton-pump inhibitor (PPIs) (e.g., omeprazole, esomeprazole)
in Trials A2201, A2301, and A2302 were 11% (N=24), 13% (N=53), and 12% (N=45),
respectively. In addition, 27 (12%), 75 (19%), and 51 (14%) subjects coadministered
remibrutinib with antacids in Trials A2201, A2301, and A2302, respectively.
Based on dose-normalized observed Cmax,ss of remibrutinib, there was no difference in exposure
to remibrutinib between subjects who coadministered remibrutinib with ARAs and subjects who
did not coadminister ARAs (Figure 15). Refer to Section 14.5.1 for details.
